1.4.

Vision, Mission, and Core Values of the University

1.4.1. Vision of the University
“to be a world class educational centre of excellence in academics,
character, sports and vocational development”
1.4.2.

Mission of the University
Afe Babalola University is a result-oriented institution for producing
highly skilled and socially relevant graduates capable of applying scientific
knowledge for the resolution of social problems. The University is equally
committed to transforming students into expert thinkers, innovative
managers and resourceful technocrats in all fields of learning.

9

1.4.3. Core goals of the University
As a unique University, it seeks to achieve the following goals:
• Encourage a balance between academic excellence, professional ethics, and practical
competence among students;
• Encourage daily consistency and personal integrity among the students. To pursue
academic excellence through outstanding teaching and the promotion of research and
community service on the national and international level. To create and promote
interdisciplinary and international scholarship and education, while sustaining
foundational disciplines;
• Define and lead emerging disciplines and intellectual paradigms that create
knowledge and to promote the translation of that knowledge to the benefit of society;
• Develop new learning opportunities that build on these paradigms and create student
engagement in the context of a community of scholars; and
• Foster effective concern for environmental well-being, justice, peace and compassion
and commitment to their promotion in the contemporary world.

1.5.

Establishment of the University
Date Established: 2009

Afe Babalola University is the brainchild of Chief Afe Babalola, B.Sc. (Econs) Lond; LL.B (Hons)
Lond; BL; FFPA; FNIALS, FCI Arb; LL.D; Senior Advocate of Nigeria (SAN); Doctor of Letters
(LL.D); Officer of the Order of the Federal Republic (OFR), and Commander of the Order of the
Niger (CON), the immediate past Pro-Chancellor and Chairman of the Governing Council of the
University of Lagos, Nigeria, and the immediate past Chairman of the Committee of ProChancellors of Nigerian Universities.
It is a product of a mind disturbed by the incurable problems afflicting government universities and
the quality of graduates being produced each year. As a Pro-chancellor and Chairman of Prochancellors, he found that these Universities problems were due to lack of commitment on behalf of
the staff and want of emotional attachment to their various institutions as in the case of a private or
personal establishment. It is common knowledge that workers in public institutions often display
lackadaisical attitude towards government work with the justification that it is not their father’s
business.
The Promoter believes that a time has come when private individuals should stand up to the
challenge of providing affordable and qualitative tertiary institutions as it is done in other nations
like the ivy league of Universities in the United States and other leading institutions in Europe.
These are classic examples of how and why private universities are more successful than
government universities evidenced by the quality of graduates they produce and their performances
in the competitive world field. For example, Japan which has the largest number of private
Universities is indisputably a leader in the field of technology which goes to show that private
10

institutions offer brighter prospects of qualitative tertiary education. This is because the proponents
of these great private universities have dreams, ideas, and objectives about the type of university
they want. They established code of conduct and disciplinary measures which are pursued
religiously and rigorously.
Location
The University is located on a 130 hectares land at an altitude of over 1500 ft above sea level along
Ado-Ekiti - Ijan Road, directly opposite the Federal Polytechnic, Ado-Ekiti in Ekiti State, which ipso
facto provides cool and ideal climate for learning and sport activities.
Objectives
In order to achieve these goals, we pursue the following objectives:
(a)

Establish in Ado Ekiti a world class institution of higher learning which
combines the acquisition of knowledge with its practical application in
training graduates of high integrity and of international recognition;

(b)

Provide an environment of international standards for learning, research,
acquisition of specialized skills and comprehensive training of students
empowering them to apply knowledge and skill to the benefit of society;

(c)

Complement the efforts of Government in bridging the yawning
unemployment gap by creating employment opportunities for Nigerians.

(d)

Develop and continuously up-date a modern library including a digital
library to encourage research, publication and to promote a “community of
learning” atmosphere, global interaction as well as promote scholarship and
conduct research in all fields of human endeavor to advance learning and
provide solutions to developmental problems for the benefit of society and
mankind;

(e)

Develop and offer specialized, flexible and innovative programmes for
specialized and non-specialized undergraduate and post graduate degrees
and non-degree programmes, thus creating a balance between academic
excellence, professional ethics and practical competence among a critical
mass of human capital who are imbued with a high sense of responsibility,
individual initiative as well as with capacity for analytical, creative and
practical thinking;

(f)

Promote effective communication among the academic community,
students and the public in order to produce specialists competent to bring
about research aimed at creative inventions and practical contribution
towards the advancement of knowledge, technological development and
for the creation of wealth for Nigeria.

11

(g)

Establish a centre for entrepreneurial studies to stimulate job creation
abilities in students from the onset of their studies;

(h)

Establish a Creative and Lifelong Education Centre for the purpose of
facilitating non-degree and vocational skills programmes;

(i)

Encourage and promote the advancement of learning and hold out to all
persons without distinction of race, creed, sex, religious or political
conviction the opportunity to acquire university education; and

(j)

Undertake other activities appropriate for teaching, research and
community service as expected of a university of high standard.

Strategies for Achieving Stated Objectives
The major University-wide strategies to be adopted in achieving the objectives of Afe Babalola
University are premised on a platform of these basic characteristics:
Assistance by way of scholarships and job opportunities on campus to brilliant but indigent
students;
*

Excellence in educational programmes that is affordable to students;

*

Commitment to attracting and retaining world-class teaching and non-teaching staff,
along with competitive employee compensation and support for success;

*

Institutional agility and resource flexibility in order to capture emerging opportunities,
and to be readily and creatively responsible to the needs of constituent stakeholders at the
state, national, and international levels.

*

Undergraduate and post-graduate programmes in various disciplines that meet the
students’ need for learning and the societal need for solutions to arts, science and
technological challenges. The University is accessible to diverse makers, and offers
curricula designed to serve their needs.

*

Courses are broad-ranged and integrated to facilitate the required creativity and
productivity of students.

*

The University emphasizes student-centered learning environments engaging academic
variety and student diversity, with the goals of providing a holistic and collaborative
approach to learning; learning through discovery, experience, and innovation; and
effective use of information.

*

The University has developed innovative means of providing financial assistance to
12

deserving indigent students, with due regard to competitive recruitment of qualified
students, cost of education and student need, enrolment characteristics, scholastic
achievement, and educational enrichment.
*

The University has developed information technology and widespread access as a catalyst
in the execution of other strategies, especially in the creation of new mechanisms for
effective engagement of service.

*

The University has created and expanded academic facilities lecture rooms, e-learning
platforms, ICT center, library, laboratories, and studios – to support innovative teaching
and learning.

*

The University provides learning experiences for students through hands-on community
service opportunities and internship opportunities that complement their career
objectives.

*

The University publicizes and promotes institutional achievements and staff and student
accomplishments among the constituent stakeholders and enhance national and
international visibility.

*

There will be formal assessment of student outcomes at the University level at least every
other year and, more frequently, there will be assessment of the outcomes and
effectiveness of academic programmes, courses, and targeted initiatives in the interest of
continuous improvement.

*

All students will take compulsory courses that cut across all programmes being
undertaken. They include courses such as Phonetics and Use of English, Communication
Skills, Logic, Creative Thinking, African Culture and Ethics, Entrepreneurship as well as
basic education in practical agriculture.

*

The University operates a non-discriminatory students’ admissions policy on the basis of
gender, tribe, religion, political leanings or physical disability. Only candidates that are
highly qualified and able to benefit from good university education will however stand a
good chance of being admitted.

*

Admission of students is through the UTME including a selection process that will from
time to time be determined by the University.

*

The method of instruction is face-to-face interaction – a normal 17 weeks semester for
roper educational impartation of knowledge. The use of modern communication facilities
available in imparting knowledge to students will be employed. Practical and tutorials
are also designed and students are given opportunities to think out projects and work
under supervision. On-line approach to student-teacher communication is in place to
facilitate student-teacher interactions. To this end every staff and student will own a
computer for their use.
13

*

There will be continuous assessment as part of the grading system to ensure that students
are up to date in their studies.

*

The University undertakes a periodic curriculum review that takes into account feedback
from organizations and employs of labour and NUC Minimum Academic Standards.

*

All students will have access to welfare, career planning and placement services, and
accommodation in well-equipped and functional halls of residence.

*

Opportunities will be provided for staff and facilities evaluation exclusively by students.

technological excellence, and produces ethical, globally competitive engineers
dedicated to solving societal challenges and advancing sustainable
development.

1.8.2. Mission of the College of Engineering Afe Babalola University
1.

To provide a dynamic and research-driven engineering education that
integrates cutting-edge technology, hands-on learning, and industry
collaboration.

2.

To develop highly skilled engineers who uphold integrity, professionalism,
and entrepreneurial spirit in addressing real-world challenges.

3.

To promote interdisciplinary research and innovation that contributes to
national and global technological advancements.

4.

To develop engineers with critical thinking, creativity, and leadership skills
for engineering solutions that drive sustainable development.

5.

To uphold the highest academic and ethical standards in training engineers
14

who will lead in various sectors of the economy.

1.14.

History of Electrical/Electronics Engineering Degree Programme

Established in September 2010 alongside three other departments within the College of
Engineering, the Electrical/Electronics and Computer Engineering department proudly offers
robust programs, one of which is B. Eng. degree in Electrical/Electronics Engineering. The
pioneering professors of the programme include Professor I. E. OWOLABI and Professor
OLOWOLAFE who led the programme to amiable heights, including medals at the Microsoft®
ImagineCupTM global competition event held in USA. This five-year program is grounded in a
strong foundation of mathematics, basic sciences, general studies, and specialized engineering
courses, designed to foster both technical expertise and ethical responsibility. Our commitment
to integrity is woven into every facet of our academic culture. We emphasize ethical engineering
practices, encouraging students to uphold honesty, accountability, and professionalism in their
work. Our seasoned staff members are equipped with diverse specializations, bringing a wealth
of expertise to the classroom, thereby guiding students toward academic excellence and
principled innovation. The programme prioritizes hands-on training to prepare students for realworld challenges. Our well-equipped laboratories, staffed by skilled technologists, provide
practical learning environments where students engage in experiments, projects, and simulations
to deepen their technical skills.
1.15.

Philosophy of Electrical/Electronic Engineering

The programme strongly believes in the production of graduates who appreciate the value of the
dignity of labor and who are problem solvers, self-reliant, employers of labor, highly productive
and globally relevant in every sphere of human endeavor.

15

1.16.
1.16.1.

Vision and Mission of the Programme
Vision of the Programme
To be a world-class center of excellence in Electrical and Electronic
Engineering education, fostering academic innovation, ethical leadership,
and holistic development in character, research, and vocational competence.

1.16.2.

Mission of the Programme
To produce highly competent Electrical and Electronic Engineers equipped
with strong technical expertise, practical skills, and professional ethics. This
will be achieved through rigorous academic training, hands-on experience,
and the development of innovative solutions to global engineering challenges,
empowering graduates to excel in their professional endeavors of applying
scientific knowledge and electrical engineering principles for the resolution of
technical and social problems.

Self-initiated Improvements of the Programme

The following are some of the measures employed for self-improvement initiative carried out by
the programme over the years:
1. Regularly assess the curriculum to ensure it aligns with current industry needs and
technological advancements.
2. Actively soliciting feedback from students on the programme, including coursework,
teaching methods, and resources.
3. Invite industry professionals to share their knowledge and experiences with students
during the career week.
4. Organize seminars on emerging technologies and industry trends to keep faculty and
students updated.
5. Encourage collaboration and knowledge sharing among students through group projects
and peer-to-peer learning activities.
6. Utilize online platforms for delivering course content, simulations, and self-paced
learning opportunities.
7. Introduce students to open-source hardware platforms like Arduino and Raspberry Pi,
and software tools for simulation and design.
8. Increase the focus on hands-on projects that require applying theoretical knowledge to
solve real-world problems. Encourage collaboration and design thinking.

SECTION TWO:
THE PROGRAMME EDUCATIONAL OBJECTIVES

23

2.3.

The Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs)

The Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs) of the Electrical/Electronics Engineering of Afe
Babalola University Ado-Ekiti is a list of concise objectives that drives the activities and
direction of the programme. These PEOs are tabulated as follows:
2.4.

2.5.

Programme Educational Objectives

PEO1

To produce highly skilled and competent Electrical/Electronics engineers that
will contribute to academia, industrial and professional Engineering practice.

PEO2

To produce proficient, self-reliant, and excellent Electrical/Electronics Engineers
equipped with strong technical skills, a solid understanding of fundamental
concepts, and the ability to address contemporary challenges in line with modern
technological developments.

PEO3

To produce graduates who will actively engage in continuous professional
development and lifelong learning, pursue advanced studies, and obtain relevant
professional certifications, thereby remaining competent and relevant in a rapidly
evolving engineering environment.

PEO4

To produce graduates who can provide practical and cost-effective solutions to
real-world problems by applying their modeling knowledge, innovative thinking,
and entrepreneurial skills, as well as their ability to design, develop, and solve
complex engineering challenges across various fields of Electrical/Electronics
Engineering.

PEO5

To produce graduates that will uphold ethical standards, acquire knowledge,
demonstrate integrity, leadership and practice Electrical/Electronics engineering
responsibly in compliance with national and international professional codes and
sustainable development principles.

Publication of the Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs)

The Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs) of Electrical and Electronic Engineering at Afe
Babalola University are documented in the official programme handbook, on the notice boards,
and on the University website www.abuad.edu.ng. They are also communicated on a regular
basis during faculty and departmental meetings, student awareness sessions, orientation
programmes, placement activities, training exercises, and through selected social media
platforms. To further ensure that graduates are well-informed, competent, and equipped with
the knowledge, skills, and mindset required in the fast-evolving world of technology, related

24

documents such as the vision, mission, motto, and Program Outcomes (POs) are shared in the
same way. These materials are also made available to all programme stakeholders for reference
and engagement.
2.5.1. Consistency of the PEOs with the Vision and Mission of the University
The Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs) are fully aligned with the Vision and Mission of
Afe Babalola University. The emphasis on producing academically minded graduates with
outstanding results in Electrical and Electronics Engineering directly supports the vision of the
University of being a world-class educational centre of excellence in academics. By fostering
technical competence, leadership, innovation, and entrepreneurship, the PEOs reflect the mission
of the University on producing highly skilled and socially relevant graduates capable of applying
scientific knowledge to solve societal problems. The integration of vocational development and
entrepreneurship skills corresponds with the University’s vision of promoting vocational
excellence and its mission to transform students into innovative managers and resourceful
technocrats. Furthermore, the focus on effective communication, collaboration, sportsmanship,
professionalism, and ethical responsibility mirrors the University’s vision of excellence in
character and sports. Finally, the PEOs’ commitment to lifelong learning, professional
development, and contributing to academia and industry reinforces the mission of developing
expert thinkers who sustain the growth and advancement of the engineering profession both
within and beyond Nigeria.

2.5.3. Consistency of PEO with stakeholders of the Programme
The PEOs have been developed in inconsistency with the different stakeholders of the
Electrical/Electronics Engineering programme. These include the students, Faculty members and
instructors, Alumni, employers, industry partners, parent forum, administrators, community and
advisory board:
26

1. Students
Students are the direct beneficiaries of the programme. They strive to acquire the knowledge,
skills, and competences that prepare them for professional careers. Their progress is
influenced by how well the Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs) are achieved. Students
are expected to understand and comply with academic regulations, instructions from
lecturers and support staff, and policies set by the university administration.
2. Faculty and Instructors
Faculty members and instructors play a central role in delivering the curriculum, designing
teaching activities, and evaluating learning outcomes. They supervise, mentor, and guide
students while aligning teaching strategies with the PEOs. Their responsibilities also include
conducting research, supervising student research projects, and ensuring that teaching
remains effective.

In addition,

technologists and instructors facilitate

laboratory

demonstrations and provide support for students during practical sessions.
3. Alumni
Alumni consist of graduates who have either advanced their studies or entered the workforce.
Their professional and academic experiences help to assess the relevance and effectiveness of
the PEOs. By sharing feedback on how well the programme prepared them for future
challenges, they contribute to continuous improvement.
4. Employers
Employers have a strong interest in the qualities and competencies of graduates, as they are
potential employees. Their assessment of graduate performance in the workplace provides
valuable insights into how closely the PEOs align with current industry expectations and
professional demands.
5. Accreditation Bodies
Accrediting organizations establish the standards and benchmarks for evaluating the quality
and effectiveness of academic programmes. They review the programme for compliance with
regulatory requirements and the extent to which the PEOs are achieved. In Nigeria, this
includes agencies such as the National Universities Commission (NUC) and the Council for
the Regulation of Engineering in Nigeria (COREN).
6. Industry Partners
Industry partners collaborate with the university by offering internships, cooperative
placements, research opportunities, and other forms of experiential learning. Their
knowledge of emerging technologies and workplace requirements influences the continuous
adaptation of the PEOs to industry realities.
7. Parents’ Forum
27

Through advocacy, constructive feedback, and active involvement, parents play a vital role
in the success of the programme. They foster stronger links between families and the
university, which positively affects student achievement and wellbeing. Parents also provide
financial support for their wards by ensuring that required fees are promptly paid.
8. Administrators
Programme administrators, including the Head of Department (HOD), university
management, governing council, and non-academic staff, oversee the overall functioning of
the programme. Their duties include resource management, evaluation, and long-term
planning. The governing council makes key policy decisions, while management ensures that
daily operations are effectively carried out. Administrative staff further support smooth
operations, thereby ensuring that the mission and vision of the institution remain aligned
with the PEOs.
9. Community and Society
The wider community benefits from the programme through the contributions of graduates
who support social development, economic growth, and innovation. Society is therefore an
important stakeholder as it gains from the knowledge and services provided by graduates.
10. Advisory Board
Advisory boards are composed of faculty members, alumni, and industry representatives
who provide expert guidance to the programme. They share perspectives on labour market
needs, industry trends, and best practices, thereby offering strategies for continuous
improvement of the programme.
2.6. Description of how the results obtained from the PEO evaluation will be used to improve
the effectiveness of the programme.
The results obtained from the evaluation of the Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs) will be
carefully analyzed to identify strengths, weaknesses, and opportunities for improvement within the
Electrical and Electronics Engineering programme. The feedback from graduates, employers, and
other stakeholders will serve as a guide for refining the curriculum to align with technological
advancements and industry needs. Emphasis will be placed on incorporating emerging areas such
as Artificial Intelligence, Renewable Energy Systems, Smart Grids, and the Internet of Things, while
also improving teaching strategies through regular staff training and exposure to modern
pedagogical methods.
Furthermore, the evaluation outcomes will be used to strengthen industry collaboration, internship
structures, and research focus to ensure graduates are well-prepared for professional practice. Areas
where performance gaps are observed, such as soft skills, ethics, and innovation, will be addressed
through mentoring, career development, and experiential learning initiatives. The entire process
28

will form part of a continuous quality improvement framework aimed at enhancing the relevance,
effectiveness, and overall excellence of the Electrical and Electronics Engineering programme.

2.7.

Types of Evaluation Surveys

The achievement of the Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs) will be evaluated every five
years. This long-term review will be supported by annual data collection through surveys and
feedback mechanisms involving students, alumni, employers, and other stakeholders. The
process will include the following approaches:
•

Graduate Surveys: Alumni will be engaged through surveys or interviews to share their
experiences and provide insights on how well the PEOs have been reflected in their
professional development.

•

Employer Feedback: Employers who have hired graduates from the programme will be
consulted to assess the quality of graduate performance and how effectively the PEOs
align with workplace demands.

•

Academic Performance Review: Student achievements will be analyzed using academic
records, project outcomes, and research contributions to evaluate their level of mastery in
key areas.

•

Programme and Course Assessments: Both courses and the overall programme will be
periodically reviewed to determine their relevance, quality, and alignment with the
established educational objectives.

•

Alumni Tracking: Records of alumni career progress and professional success will be
monitored to evaluate the long-term influence of the programme on graduates and their
contributions to industry and society.

•

Advisory Board Input: Advisory boards, together with a follow-up team that includes
faculty members, will use available platforms such as social media to provide guidance,
recommendations, and independent evaluations.

•

Student Feedback: Current students will provide feedback through surveys, interviews,
interactive sessions, and continuous course evaluations to capture their perspectives on
programme delivery and outcomes.

2.8.

Frequency of PEO Review

PEOs are to be reviewed regularly every FIVE years based on the reports from various surveys
carried out.
29

2.9.

Evaluation of Level of Achievement of PEOs
•

Data Collection: Information relevant to the achievement of PEOs will be gathered from
multiple sources, including the surveys and feedback mechanisms outlined in Section 2.6.

•

Assessment of Alignment: The collected data will be analyzed to determine how well it
reflects the intended outcomes of each PEO. Graduate performance will be compared
against predefined indicators to establish the extent of achievement.

•

Strengths and Areas for Improvement: A SWOT (Strengths, Weaknesses, Opportunities,
and Threats) analysis will be employed to highlight areas of strong attainment as well as
aspects needing attention. This periodic analysis helps identify patterns of success and
opportunities for targeted improvement.

•

Continuous Improvement: Findings from the evaluation will inform curriculum updates,
teaching methods, and programme modifications. Stakeholders such as faculty, students,
alumni, and employers will be engaged in this process to ensure the programme remains
relevant and effective.

•

Documentation and Communication: The evaluation procedures and outcomes will be
formally documented to ensure accountability. Results will be shared with key
stakeholders—including faculty, students, accrediting bodies, parent forum, and industry
partners—to reinforce the programme’s commitment to quality assurance and ongoing
improvement.

•

Advisory Board Meetings: Quarterly meetings with the departmental advisory board,
conducted either virtually through platforms such as Zoom or Google Meet, will be held
to review PEO attainment levels and provide strategic input.

•

Student Engagement: Meetings between academic level advisers and the Head of
Programme will be encouraged to gather direct feedback from students on the quality and
delivery of lectures.

2.11.

Overall Survey Rating (OSR)

OSR provides a concise and standardized representation of stakeholders' perceptions and
experiences, offering valuable insights for evaluation, improvement, and decision-making
purposes.

2.12.

Review of the academic curriculum – Continuous Quality Improvement (CQI)

Strategies

The CQI strategies to be implemented in relationship with PEOs are as follows.
1. Regular Assessment and Data Collection: The programme will put in place a
methodical procedure to gather information regarding PEO achievement. This could
include the previously mentioned surveys, interviews, employer assessments, comments
from alumni, academic performance indicators, and other pertinent information sources.
The degree of success attained by each PEO will be determined by analyzing and
assessing this data. Based on the evaluation results, trends, strengths, weaknesses, and
opportunities for improvement will be closely followed.
2. Stakeholder Engagement: The assessment process will engage stakeholders including
employers,

industrial partners,

educators, students,

alumni,

and community

representatives. Get their opinion on the PEOs' applicability and efficacy as well as
suggestions for development.
3. Curriculum Review and Revision: The assessment findings lead to an inform
curriculum review and revision efforts. This will lead to identification of opportunities
to enhance the curriculum, instructional methods, learning resources, and assessment
32

tools to better align with the PEOs and meet the evolving needs of stakeholders.
4. Faculty/Faculty Development: Provide professional development opportunities for
Faculty/faculty to enhance their teaching effectiveness, incorporate best practices, and
align instructional strategies with the PEOs. Encourage collaboration and sharing of
expertise among Faculty/faculty members. Organize more conferences, seminars, and
workshops. Staff are also encouraged and to attend yearly COREN assembly, Nigeria
Society of Engineers yearly workshop both locally and nationally to learn about current
progress in Engineering profession.
5. Student Support and Advising: Implement support mechanisms to help students
achieve the PEOs, such as academic level advising, mentoring programs, career
counseling, and opportunities for experiential learning. Foster a supportive and inclusive
learning environment that encourages student engagement and success.

6. Continuous Monitoring through Quality Assurance (QA): The University has put in
place a quality assurance team in collaboration with the programme to establish a
feedback loop to monitor the effectiveness of quality improvement efforts and adjust as
needed. Regularly review assessment data, solicit feedback from stakeholders, and track
progress towards achieving the PEOs. It is a committee committed to fostering good

ethics and best practices.
7. Documentation and Reporting: The results of quality improvement efforts, including
assessment findings, action plans, and outcomes, will be documented and
communicated transparently to stakeholders about progress, challenges, and
accomplishments related to the PEOs.
8. Continuous Improvement: These are done through thorough analysis in regular board
meetings. This will foster a culture of continuous improvement within the programme
emphasizing the importance of ongoing assessment, reflection, and adaptation, to ensure
that quality and relevancy of the programme is not tampered with.

33

SECTION THREE:
THE PROGRAMME OUTCOMES (POs)

34

3.1.

Programme Outcomes (POs)

The Programme Outcomes (POs) for the Electrical/Electronics Engineering Programme at Afe
Babalola University are presented in Table 3.1. They are carefully structured statements that
represent the proficiencies we expect of our students by the time they graduate.
Consistency of PO’s with COREN Twelve (12) POs
The Programme Outcomes of the Electrical/Electronics Engineering program (twelve in number)
is consistent with COREN’s Programme Outcomes in the area of engineering knowledge,
problem analysis, design/development of solutions, investigation and modern tool usage. Other
areas of consistency include the engineer and society, environment and sustainability, ethics,
individual and team work, communication, project management and lifelong learning.
Table 3.1: Programme Outcomes
PO1

Engineering
Knowledge

Apply knowledge of mathematics, science, engineering fundamentals
and an engineering specialization to the solution of developmental and
complex engineering problems.

PO2

Problem Analysis Identify, formulate, research literature, and analyze developmental and
complex engineering problems reaching substantiated conclusions using
first principles of mathematics, natural sciences and engineering sciences

PO3

Design/Develop
Proffer solutions for developmental or complex engineering problems
ment of Solutions and design systems, components or processes that meet specified needs
with appropriate consideration for public health and safety, cultural,
societal, and environmental considerations

PO4

Investigation

Conduct investigation into developmental or complex problems using
research-based knowledge and research methods including design of
experiments, analysis and interpretation of data, and synthesis of
information to provide valid conclusions

PO5

Modern Tool
Usage

Create, select, and apply appropriate techniques, resources and modern
engineering and ICT tools, including prediction, modeling, and
optimization to developmental and complex engineering activities, with
an understanding of the limitations

in independent and lifelong learning in the broadest context of
technological and social changes.

3.3.

Relationship of Programme Outcomes to Programme Educational Objectives:
1. The programme outcomes equip students with the basic principles of Electrical and
Electronic Engineering, serving as a strong foundation for building a career at the highest
level of professional expertise.
2. It enhances the ability of students to apply technical knowledge while fostering analytical
thinking, innovative design skills, creativity, adaptability, and leadership capacity.
3. It offers students the chance to expand their knowledge after gaining a general
understanding of all aspects of Electrical and Electronic Engineering practice, with
opportunities to specialize in fields such as Telecommunication Systems, Electronics,
Embedded Systems, Control Systems, Power Systems, Renewable Energy, Power
Electronics,

Energy

Management

and

Electrical

Machines,

and

Electromagnetic Fields and Waves.
4. It delivers hands-on training within industries and Electrical and Electronic Engineering
establishments, preparing students for effective professional practice. All the twelve
programme outcomes are coded PO1 – PO12 and used to map the programme
educational objectives coded PEO1 – PEO5. This is presented in Table 3.3. The programme
outcomes were developed to link that of the regulatory body for engineering education in
Nigeria (COREN) Furthermore, these programme outcomes have been adequately
directed to achieve the desired impact of the curriculum in fulfilling the programme
educational objectives.

Description of the PO definition or elements/performance indicators

i. Direct approaches for assessing Program Outcomes (POs) focus on clearly evaluating the
knowledge and abilities of students in relation to defined performance indicators. These methods
provide tangible proof of how well students have achieved the expected learning goals and how
prepared they are to meet professional requirements. Such direct assessments include
examinations, quizzes, assignments, laboratory work, project reports, performance appraisals,
and final year or capstone projects.

38

ii. The assessment process is designed to mirror as closely as possible the real tasks that students
are expected to handle as engineers or professionals, including research projects, laboratory
activities, oral presentations, and related academic exercises.
3.5.

Description of the processes used to establish and review the POs,

and the extent to which the program’s various stakeholders are involved in these processes
(where applicable). This includes describing the tools used in the processes (survey, meetings,
interviews, etc.) and frequency of activities and timelines
The feedback and evaluation tools at the end of a four-year cycle are hereafter described:
i. Employer Feedback Survey: This survey collects insights from employers regarding the
performance, competencies, and workforce readiness of graduates. It highlights how effectively
the program aligns with industry expectations and professional requirements.
ii. Alumni Feedback Survey: This survey gathers responses from graduates about their learning
experiences, career development, and the practical relevance of their academic training. Alumni
input helps measure how well the program meets its long-term educational objectives.
iii. Employment Statistics: This record tracks the employment status and career progress of
graduates, including job placements, advanced studies, and professional achievements. It
provides quantitative evidence of the program’s success in supporting graduates’ career growth
and academic advancement.
iv. Industry Advisory Board: This board, consisting of professionals and industry stakeholders,
offers advice, recommendations, and feedback to ensure the program remains current and
relevant. Their expertise in industry trends, new technologies, and skill requirements contributes
to ongoing curriculum updates and enhancements.
•

Effectiveness Criteria
Survey Satisfaction: A minimum of 50 percent of survey responses must score 3 or above
on a 5-point scale, showing that participants are satisfied with program outcomes and
graduate performance.
Graduate Success: At least 50 percent of graduates should either be employed or pursuing
advanced studies, demonstrating the program’s effectiveness in preparing students for
professional careers or further education.

3.6.

Description of the mapping of courses with POs

The connection between Course Learning Outcomes (CLOs) and Program Outcomes (POs) is
structured to effectively support the overall objectives of the program. To ensure adequate
coverage of competencies, at least half of the mapped courses must demonstrate attainment of

39

the related POs. In addition, a minimum of 60 percent of student feedback should score 3 or above
on a five-point scale, showing satisfactory achievement of the expected learning outcomes.
This linkage of CLOs to POs is incorporated into the course compact for every course. Through
this process, instructors clearly specify how the learning objectives of their courses contribute to
the wider goals of the program. Embedding this mapping into course documentation promotes
transparency and ensures proper alignment between course content, intended learning outcomes,
and program outcomes.
3.7 Application of assessment results to further develop and improve the POs.
The results from assessments are used for the continuous improvement of the following:

3.8.

•

Curriculum

•

Lecture delivery methods

•

Student participation levels in class
Description of the materials that demonstrate achievement of the POs

SWEP and SIWES logbooks, laboratory reports, and project reports, along with assessments such
as assignments, tests, and examination booklets, serve as tangible evidence demonstrating the
fulfilment or achievement of the Program Outcomes (POs).
3.9.

Description of the extent to which the programme’s various stakeholders are involved
in the processes.

The roles of the stakeholder in the program processes include:
i. Alumni and Graduate Feedback: Graduates provide meaningful perspectives that help refine
both the curriculum and institutional practices. Their input highlights strengths and areas for
improvement, ensuring the program stays relevant and continues to prepare students effectively
for professional practice.
ii. Institutional Participation: The institution plays a central role in shaping and updating the
curriculum. Adjustments are guided by industry developments, evolving academic standards,
and recommendations from key stakeholders, thereby enriching the overall learning experience.
iii. Industry Advisory Boards: These boards ensure that the Electrical/Electronics Engineering
curriculum reflects current industry expectations. Their contributions support alignment
between academic training and workplace requirements, equipping students with essential
knowledge and skills.
iv. Employer Assessments: Feedback from employers offers an external measure of how well
academic preparation translates into workplace performance. These evaluations reveal strengths
and gaps in graduate competencies, guiding curriculum adjustments that enhance employability.
40

v. Faculty Development: Continuous professional growth among faculty members strengthens
teaching quality and directly benefits students. By advancing their knowledge and skills, faculty
ensure that learning outcomes remain consistent with program goals and industry standards.
3.10. Describe how the results obtained from the assessment will be applied to further
develop and improve the POs
The results obtained from the assessment of the Programme Outcomes (POs) will be
systematically analyzed to determine the extent to which students are achieving the expected
knowledge, skills, and attitudes outlined in the Electrical/Electronics Engineering curriculum.
Areas of strong performance will be consolidated and used as benchmarks, while identified gaps
will guide targeted curriculum revisions, teaching enhancements, and laboratory upgrades.
Feedback from students, faculty, alumni, and industry partners will also be integrated to ensure
that the POs remain relevant to current engineering practices, emerging technologies, and
accreditation standards.
Furthermore, the assessment outcomes will inform continuous improvement strategies such as
faculty development programmes, pedagogical innovations, and interdisciplinary project
integration to strengthen the linkage between theoretical knowledge and practical applications.
The results will also support the realignment of course objectives and assessment methods to
better reflect the POs, ensuring that graduates are well-prepared for professional practice,
research, and innovation. Overall, the findings will drive a culture of evidence-based
improvement, sustaining the quality and global competitiveness of the Electrical and Electronics
Engineering programme.
3.11.

Description of Continuous Quality Improvement (CQI) strategies to be implemented

in relation to POs
Continuous Quality Improvement (CQI) strategies include:
i. External Examiner Evaluation: Outcome-Based Education (OBE) appraisal forms are employed
by external examiners to evaluate examination questions and prepare detailed reports. This
practice upholds assessment standards and promotes continuous program enhancement.
ii. Lecture Monitoring: Lectures are regularly observed as part of a structured quality
improvement strategy. Although specific processes may vary, the monitoring typically involves
systematic review of teaching methods to highlight strengths and identify areas needing
refinement.
iii. Curriculum Review: The program curriculum is reviewed every five years. Two reviews have
been completed to date, and the current third review (CCMAS) allows the department to
contribute thirty percent of its content. This cycle ensures that the curriculum stays updated,
relevant, and aligned with contemporary academic and industry requirements.

41

iv. Facilities Upgrades: Continuous investments are made in upgrading infrastructure, including
the acquisition of smart boards and advanced laboratory equipment. These improvements create
a modern learning environment that enhances both teaching and student learning experiences.
v. Faculty Development: Faculty members engage in ongoing professional growth through
workshops, seminars, and training sessions. These activities keep them informed about new
teaching methods, innovations in pedagogy, and technological advances, ultimately improving
instructional quality and student outcomes.
vi. End of semester surveys: Students are asked to submit a survey and ratings of key indices for
the performances of their lecturers.

3.12. Policies, Internal Processes and Practices that are in Place at all Levels Within the
Institution Relating to the Accreditation Criteria
The following are some of the policies and processes put in place by the programme to ensure
compliance with COREN requirements:
a) Quality Assurance Committee: The Electrical/Electronics programme utilizes a dedicated

committee composed of professors and senior academic staff to oversee instructional
quality. This committee evaluates the performance of lecturers and technologists every
semester and recommends improvements to university management.
b) Exam Vetting Committee: Standardized assessment is maintained by a vetting committee

that reviews all examination questions for 100 to 400-level courses through senior coexaminers. Final approval is conducted by the Head of Department as Chief Examiner.
c)

External Examiner Implementation: The department appoints an approved External
Examiner, currently Prof. Jide J. Popoola, to moderate final-year examination questions,
marking guides, and projects. Feedback from moderation reports is analyzed during
board meetings to implement remedial actions.

d) COREN Recommendations:

Following the previous accreditation exercise, the
department successfully implemented key recommendations by restructuring laboratory
sessions so that each is coordinated by a lecturer alongside technologists and by increasing
the number of registered technical staff.

Criterion 1: Programme Educational Objectives (PEOs)
The PEOs (PEO1–PEO5) define the long-term goals for graduates, such as contributing to
professional engineering practice, solving contemporary technological challenges, and upholding
high ethical standards. These objectives are published in handbooks, on notice boards, and on the

42

university website. They are reviewed every five years using feedback from alumni, employers,
and industry partners.
Criterion 2: Programme Outcomes (POs)
The programme has adopted 12 POs that are fully aligned with the COREN and Washington
Accord attributes. These outcomes ensure graduates possess the necessary engineering
knowledge, problem-analysis skills, and modern tool usage capabilities required for professional
practice.
Criterion 3: Course Learning Outcomes (CLOs)
Every course is defined by specific, measurable CLOs that are mapped to the programme
outcomes within individual Course Compacts. This mapping ensures that the mastery of coursespecific content directly contributes to the achievement of the broader graduation requirements.
Criterion 4: Curriculum and Learning Process
The curriculum is built on an Outcome-Based Education (OBE) framework and uses a
Modularised Course Unit System. It integrates 30% local content specialization through
"signature courses" designed to address regional engineering needs. The learning process
combines traditional lectures with Problem-Based Learning (PBL) and mandatory practical
exposure.
Criterion 5: Students
Admission follows COREN regulation, requiring five O'Level credits (including Mathematics,
Physics, and Chemistry) for UTME or relevant diplomas for Direct Entry. Students are guided by
Academic Level Advisers and must maintain a minimum of 80% attendance to be eligible for final
examinations.
Criterion 6: Continuous Quality Improvement (CQI)
CQI strategies involve systematic curriculum reviews every five years and annual data collection
from various stakeholders. The department uses employer satisfaction ratings and graduate
performance data to refine laboratory facilities and teaching methods.
Criterion 7: Staffing
The department maintains a strong academic faculty, with 11 out of 15 (73.3%) dedicated staff
holding doctoral degrees. Over 90% of the academic staff are registered professional engineers
with COREN. The university supports staff development through Ph.D. sponsorship and
sponsorship for the annual COREN assembly.
Criterion 8: Physical Facilities and Infrastructures

43

Learning is supported by nine specialized laboratories, including the Electrical Machine, Power
and Energy Lab, and the National Instruments Lab, all equipped with advanced workstations.
Facilities include smart boards, high-speed WiFi, and a departmental library with a capacity for
80 students.
Criterion 9: Institutional Linkage and Community Service
Strong industrial linkages are managed through the Industry Advisory Board, which provides
feedback on curriculum relevance. Students gain mandatory real-world experience through 24
weeks of SIWES industrial exposure at recognized firms such as the Transmission Company of
Nigeria or TransAfam Power.
Criterion 10: Institutional Support and Funding
The university provides consistent financial commitment for faculty salaries, research grants, and
regular infrastructure maintenance. Resources are allocated annually for laboratory re-tooling
and the acquisition of modern engineering software (e.g., MATLAB, Altium) to maintain
accreditation readiness.
3.13. Involvement of industry in discussions and fora, professional practice exposure, and
collaborative projects/research for the solutions to engineering problems.
The Electrical/Electronics Engineering programme maintains strong industry ties to ensure that
its students are well-prepared for professional practice and equipped with current technological
skills.
3.13.1. Involvement in Discussions and Fora
The department actively engages with industry stakeholders to keep the curriculum and teaching
methods aligned with modern engineering requirements:
a) Industry Advisory Board: This board, comprised of seasoned professionals and industry

experts, provides guidance on labor market needs, emerging technological trends, and
curriculum enhancements to ensure graduates are workplace-ready.
b) Career Weeks and Seminars: Industry professionals are invited annually to share real-

world knowledge and industrial experiences during Career Week. Additionally, guest
seminars and invited lectures from various engineering professional associations are
integrated into the Engineer in Society (GET 101) course.
3.13.2. Professional Practice Exposure and Certifications
Exposure to professional practice is facilitated through specialized certifications and extensive
industrial placements:

44

a) Professional Certifications (New Horizons): Through a partnership with New Horizons,

the department integrates professional IT and engineering certifications directly into the
curriculum:
i)

CCNA (ABUAD-EEE-102) at the 100 level.

ii) Python Programming (ABUAD-EEE-202) at the 200 level.
iii) Machine Learning (ABUAD-EEE-302) at the 300 level.
iv) Embedded System/IoT (ABUAD-EEE-502) at the 500 level.
b) Industrial Placements (United Nigeria Airlines): Students gain deep industrial exposure

through the Students Industrial Work Experience Scheme (SIWES). A notable example of
this partnership is United Nigeria Airlines, which has provided industrial training and
exposure for as many as 25 students from the programme.
3.13.3. Collaborative Projects, Research, and Awards
Industry collaboration is central to the department’s research and student recognition efforts:
a) Industry-Sponsored Projects: Final-year students are encouraged to undertake projects

that tackle complex engineering challenges sourced directly from industrial practice. This
is managed by the departmental Research and Workshop Committee to foster applied
research and innovation.
b) Excellence Awards (Dorman Long Engineering): To motivate students and promote

professional standards, Dorman Long Engineering Nigeria Limited provides several
awards for different categories of graduating students, recognizing academic excellence
and innovative engineering potential.

Course Overview and description
This course covers the basics of programming language such as variables, data types, expressions
assignment statements, conditional and iterative control structures with emphases to Java
programming language. Other key aspects such as Control structures, arrays, methods; pointers,

tailored towards Object Oriented (OOP) paradigm in Java. Object Oriented Programming concepts
such objects, classes and subclasses, Inheritance, encapsulation, data abstraction and Polymorphism
are well covered in the course. The course addresses the structure of graphical user interface (GUI),
with specific application to java programming language.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)

Course Code: EEE 221

Course Title: Basic Electrical Engineering I

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Engineering Auditiorium

Lecturer(s):

Engr.

Textbooks and other Materials:
⚫

Elements of Electromagnetics by N. O. Sadiku

⚫

Network Theory by C. K. Alexander and M. N. O. Sadiku Jean-Pierre

⚫

Measurement and Instrumentation Principle by Alan S. Morris

a)

Course Overview and description

Basic Electrical Engineering I introduces students to the fundamental principles and applications
of electrical engineering, serving as a foundation for advanced courses in circuits,
electromagnetics, machines, and power systems. The course begins with an exploration of electric
and magnetic fields, covering Coulomb’s law, Gauss’s law, Biot–Savart law, and Ampere’s law,
52

before progressing into the concepts of magnetic circuits, magnetomotive force, permeability, and
B–H curves. Students also study lumped circuit elements, resistors, capacitors, and inductors,
alongside their voltage–current characteristics and roles in energy storage and dissipation. Core
circuit analysis techniques are introduced through network theorems such as Kirchhoff’s laws,
Thevenin’s and Norton’s theorems, Superposition, and Delta–Star transformations. The course
further develops analytical skills through the study of AC circuits, including single-phase and
three-phase systems, impedance, phasors, resonance, power factor, and load balancing under
both balanced and unbalanced conditions. Emphasis is placed on applying mathematical tools to
real engineering problems, interpreting results, and evaluating circuit performance. By
combining theoretical concepts with problem-solving practice, the course equips students with
the skills needed to analyze, model, and assess electrical systems while building competencies in
engineering reasoning, teamwork, communication, and professional growth as outlined in the
programme outcomes.
b) Pre-requisites: Nil
c) Co-requisite(s): Nil
d) Role in Curriculum: Core
e) Course Learning Outcome (CLO) and Programme Outcomes (PO)

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments are designed to challenge students to think critically

and solve problems in the world of electromagnetism, analyze real-world scenarios and propose
solutions using the acquired knowledge of electromagnetic principles. The course also includes
assignments designed to solidify your understanding through calculations, problem-solving
exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving electromagnetic phenomena.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

This course is highly relevant to today’s rapidly evolving power and electronics industry. A solid
grasp of electric and magnetic fields, network theorems, and AC power systems underpins
modern technologies such as renewable energy integration, smart grids, and efficient industrial
power distribution. The course equips students with analytical and problem-solving skills
essential for addressing challenges like energy efficiency, sustainable power generation, and
reliable communication systems. Its concepts directly apply in industries ranging from power
58

engineering and electronics manufacturing to automation, ICT, and emerging renewable
technologies.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)
College of Engineering
Department of Electrical/Electronics and Computer Engineering
Course Code: EEE 222

Course Title: Basic Electrical Engineering II

Semester: Second

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Engineering Auditiorium Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M. O. Onibonoje, Mr. Victor
Akinpelu
Textbooks and other Materials:
⚫

Electricity and Magnetism by T. Matsushita

⚫

Basic Network Theory by J. Vlach

⚫

Integrated Electronics by J. Millman and C. Holkias

a)

Course Overview and description

Basic Electrical Engineering II builds upon the foundational concepts introduced in Basic
Electrical Engineering I, extending students’ understanding from classical circuit theory into the
domain of semiconductor physics and electronic devices. The course begins with an introduction
to the atomic structure and the fundamental principles governing semiconductors, including the
behavior of charge carriers, effective mass, mobility, conductivity, carrier lifetime, and
recombination phenomena. Students are then introduced to transport mechanisms such as drift,
diffusion, continuity equations, and the Hall effect, which provide the basis for analyzing
semiconductor behavior in practical applications. The course progresses to the study of PN
junction materials, diodes, and their practical uses in rectification, filtering, and voltage
regulation circuits, with emphasis on Zener diode applications. Building on this, transistor theory
is explored in depth, covering bipolar junction transistors (BJT) in common-base, commonemitter, and common-collector configurations, as well as field-effect transistors (FETs). Their
static and dynamic characteristics are analyzed, and their role as switching devices and amplifiers

59

is emphasized. Students will also engage with small-signal amplifier design and AC/DC load line
analysis to strengthen both analytical and design skills. By the end of the course, students are
expected to not only understand the theoretical principles of semiconductor devices but also
apply them in designing, analyzing, and interpreting basic electronic circuits, thereby bridging
the gap between circuit theory and modern electronics. This prepares students for advanced
courses in electronics and equips them with problem-solving and design competencies relevant
to real-world electrical engineering applications.
b) Pre-requisites: Nil
c) Co-requisite(s): Nil
d) Role in Curriculum: Core

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments are designed to challenge students to think critically

and solve problems in the world of electromagnetism, analyze real-world scenarios and propose
solutions using the acquired knowledge of electromagnetic principles. The course also includes
assignments designed to solidify your understanding through calculations, problem-solving
exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving electromagnetic phenomena.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Basic Electrical Engineering II is highly relevant in today’s technology-driven world, where
semiconductors and electronic devices form the backbone of modern industries. From consumer

66

electronics and renewable energy systems to telecommunications and automation, the principles
of semiconductor physics, PN junctions, transistors, and amplifiers are central to innovation.
With the rapid growth of microelectronics, VLSI, and nanotechnology, engineers require strong
foundational knowledge to address issues of miniaturization, efficiency, and sustainability. This
course equips students with the skills to contribute to advancements in electronics, power
systems, and emerging fields like IoT and smart devices, ensuring industrial and societal impact.

Course Overview and description

This course covers the fundamentals of electromechanical devices and transformers, including
energy conversion principles, transformer performance and types, DC machine operation,
excitation, characteristics, and applications. It emphasizes practical aspects such as
motor/generator selection, speed control, and braking, preparing students to analyze and apply
machines and transformers in real engineering systems.

Group works and Assignments
Coursework requires students to analyze the behavior of transformers and DC machines,

differentiating their types and examining their characteristics. Through collaborative and
individual assignments, students apply principles of electromechanical energy conversion to
evaluate performance and select appropriate machines for practical applications, thereby
solidifying their ability to describe and explain fundamental operations.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
The course equips students to analyze the performance of transformers and DC machines

and evaluate their suitability for real-world applications, emphasizing reliable, efficient, and safe
operation. Through the application of design and selection principles, students learn to propose
robust solutions that meet operational constraints, supporting sustainable engineering practices.

Course Code: EEE 315

Course Title: Electromagnetic Fields and Waves I

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Three hours lectures per week
Location: Hall C3-6

Lecturer(s): Engr. Dr. A. O. Ojo

Textbooks and other Materials:
- Owolabi & Ojo (2022), Electromagnetic Fields and Waves – Principles and Applications
Engineering Electromagnetics, 1st edition, Matprints Inc.
- Gunther Lehner (2010), Electromagnetic Field Theory for Engineers and Physicists, Springer
Heidelberg Dordrecht London New York
a)

Course Overview and description

This course covers the fundamental concepts of electromagnetics, emphasizing the mathematical
tools and physical principles essential for analyzing and solving problems in electrical
engineering. Topics include vector analysis, integral theorems, and the behavior of electric and
magnetic fields governed by laws such as Coulomb’s Law, Gauss’s Law, Faraday’s Law, BiotSavart and Ampere’s Law. The course covers electric fields and electric potentials for various
charge distributions, including lines, rings, surfaces, volumes, and electric dipoles. A key focus is
on Maxwell’s Equations and their role in understanding electromagnetic wave propagation in
various media, as well as practical applications such as wave behavior, reflection, refraction, and
transmission, and modern communication systems and their societal and ethical impacts.
b)

Pre-requisites: Nil

c)

Co-requisite(s): Nil

d)

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)
College of Engineering
Department of Electrical/Electronics and Computer Engineering
Course Code: EEE 319

Course Title: Measurement and Instrumentation

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Engineering Auditorium Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M.O Onibonoje, Mr. Victor
Akinpelu
Textbooks and other Materials:
⚫

Measurement and Instrumentation Principle by Alan S. Morris

⚫

Electrical Technology by B. L. Theraja and A.K. Theraja

⚫

A Course in Electronic and Electrical Measurement & Instrumentation by Gupta

⚫

Basic Concepts of Medical Instrumentation by Walter H. Olson

a)

Course Overview and description

Measurement and Instrumentation introduces students to the fundamental principles,
techniques, and tools essential for accurate measurement in electrical and electronic engineering.
The course provides a solid grounding in units, symbols, and standards of measurement,
followed by a study of the theory of errors, distinguishing between systematic and random errors
and methods for minimizing their impact on results. Students will explore a wide range of
instruments, beginning with traditional indicating devices such as moving coil, moving iron,
dynamometer, and electrostatic instruments, before progressing to advanced measuring systems
80

for DC and AC quantities, including impedance, power, and energy measurements. The use of
instrument potentiometers, oscilloscopes, and cathode-ray tubes (CRT) for measurement
applications forms a crucial part of the curriculum, along with the study of pen recorders and
modern digital test instruments. In addition, the course introduces students to practical issues
such as signal interference, screening, and grounding techniques that ensure accuracy and
reliability in measurement systems. Through this comprehensive coverage, students develop the
ability to identify and understand key electrical quantities, interpret measurement results, and
effectively apply modern test equipment in experimental and industrial contexts. The course
outcomes emphasize both conceptual understanding and practical application, aligning with
broader programme learning objectives that encourage problem-solving, communication, ethical
responsibility, and the integration of technology in engineering practice. Ultimately, EEE 319
equips students with the essential skills to analyze, interpret, and communicate measurement
data, preparing them for advanced studies and professional practice in electrical and electronic
engineering.

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Measurement and Instrumentation is highly relevant to contemporary engineering practice, as
accurate measurement underpins technological innovation, industrial automation, and quality

86

assurance. In modern industries such as power systems, telecommunications, and electronics
manufacturing, reliable instrumentation ensures efficiency, safety, and compliance with
international standards. With the growing integration of digital test instruments, smart sensors,
and real-time monitoring systems, engineers must adapt to rapidly evolving technologies. This
course equips students with the skills to address these industrial needs, fostering competence in
error analysis, data interpretation, and advanced measurement tools essential for tackling present
and future engineering challenges.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)

Course Code: EEE 324

Course Title: Linear systems

Semester: Second

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Hall B2-5

Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M. O. Onibonoje, Mr. Victor

Akinpelu
Textbooks and other Materials:
◼

Linear System by H. Bourles

◼

Control System by Norman Nise

◼

Feedback and Control Systems by Joseph J. DiStefano, Allen R. Stubberud and Ivan J.
Williams

a)

Course Overview and description

Linear Systems provides students with a comprehensive foundation in the modeling, analysis,
and evaluation of dynamic systems across electrical, mechanical, and thermal domains. The
course begins by introducing mathematical models of physical systems and develops the concept
of system analogies, enabling learners to translate problems from one physical domain to another.
Emphasis is placed on control system classifications and feedback concepts, highlighting their
role in shaping system performance and ensuring desired outputs under varying conditions.

87

Students explore transfer functions, block diagrams, and signal flow graphs, building the skills
to represent, simplify, and interconnect systems efficiently. The course further investigates
system responses, covering both transient and steady-state behaviors, and equips students with
frequency-domain analysis tools such as root-locus, Bode plots, Nyquist diagrams, and polar
plots for performance characterization and design insights. Stability analysis forms a crucial
component, with techniques such as Routh-Hurwitz, Nyquist, and Bode criteria applied to
determine robustness and reliability of systems. Finally, an introduction to analogue computer
simulation enhances practical understanding by demonstrating system behavior and control
strategies. By the end of the course, students will be able to model real-world systems, analyze
responses, assess stability, and apply feedback principles, thereby gaining a strong analytical base
for advanced courses in control engineering, signal processing, and system design. The
knowledge gained equips learners to tackle contemporary industrial challenges where system
modeling and control are vital, from automation and robotics to energy systems and
communication networks.
b) Pre-requisites: Nil
c) Co-requisite(s): Nil
d) Role in Curriculum: Core

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

93

Linear systems form the backbone of modern engineering applications, from automation and
robotics to power and communication networks. With industries increasingly reliant on control,
modeling, and stability analysis for reliable and efficient operations, knowledge of system
dynamics is crucial. This course equips students with tools to analyze performance, ensure
stability, and optimize feedback in real-world systems. Contemporary challenges such as
renewable energy integration, smart grids, autonomous vehicles, and advanced manufacturing
highlight the industrial relevance of linear systems, making the course essential for addressing
emerging technological and sustainability demands.

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments in Circuit Theory I are designed to challenge students

to think critically and solve problems within the domain of electrical circuits, encouraging
analysis of real-world scenarios and the application of circuit principles to propose effective
solutions. These tasks also reinforce students’ understanding through calculations, analytical
exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving modern circuit configurations
and network behaviors.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
Electrical circuit theory faces modern challenges such as miniaturization, energy

efficiency, and integration with smart technologies like IoT and intelligent control systems.
Engineers must also address cybersecurity in embedded systems and rely on advanced
simulation tools to model increasingly complex circuit behaviors. This course is vital across
industries including power, telecommunications, consumer electronics, and automotive, where
circuit design directly impacts system performance and reliability. From optimizing energy
distribution to enabling smart devices and automation, circuit theory remains a cornerstone of
innovation and engineering practice.

Course Overview and description

This course introduces the fundamental principles of reliability, maintainability, and availability
(RMA) with a focus on electrical and electronic systems, including power systems. Students will
learn elementary reliability theory and its practical applications to the design, testing, and
maintenance of electronic components and systems. Topics include, fault types and analysis,
design strategies for enhanced reliability, and environmental considerations. The course
emphasizes testing methods and real-world applications to prepare students for challenges in
engineering design and system maintenance.

Group works and Assignments
Group work and assignments in this course are structured to deepen students’

understanding of reliability engineering concepts through collaborative analysis and hands-on
application. Teams evaluate system and component failure probabilities using reliability theory,
interpret fault types for improvement strategies, and apply reliability principles to power and
electronic systems. Assignments include testing and validating electrical components, and
designing systems for enhanced reliability. This approach fosters teamwork, critical thinking, and
practical problem-solving aligned with industry standards.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
This course helps develop in the students, essential skills for designing reliable,

maintainable, and available systems; equipping would be engineers to predict failures, analyze
faults, and apply reliability principles to power and electronic systems. Valid testing and robust
design practices ensure compliance, safety, and performance. Together, the outcomes support
sustainable engineering and foster lifelong learning in a rapidly evolving field.

Renewable Energy Systems

Semester:

a)

Course Overview and description

This course provides an overview of the modern energy landscape, covering conventional and
non-conventional energy sources, energy efficiency, energy security, and distributed generation.
It explores solar energy systems—both thermal and photovoltaic—focusing on PV characteristics,
equivalent circuits, efficiency, sizing, economics, and peak power operation. Wind energy is
addressed through wind power relations, turbine types and ratings, generator selection, variable
speed operation, and control strategies. The course also examines grid integration of renewable
systems, including stand-alone, hybrid, and micro-grid configurations, with emphasis on
economics, interface requirements, stable operation, and impacts on power quality such as
harmonics, transients, flicker, and reactive power.

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments in this course are central to achieving the course

learning outcome: assessing energy sources for sustainable solutions. Through collaborative
projects, students learn to diagnose real-world energy inefficiency issues, conceptualize
distributed generation systems, and evaluate energy storage options. The individual assignments
help solidify understanding through calculations in electromechanical conversion, analysis of

set for sustainable energy problem-solving.

by providing a deep understanding of electromechanical systems and sustainable solutions. The
curriculum is designed around industry-relevant challenges, focusing on diagnosing energy
inefficiencies, designing distributed generation systems for grid resilience, and evaluating
advanced storage technologies. Students will learn to propose viable solutions for industrial
decarbonization, waste heat recovery, and the integration of renewables, preparing them to
contribute directly to the global energy transition..

Course Code: EEE 566

Course Title: Non-Linear systems

Semester: Second

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Hall B2-5

Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M.O Onibonoje, Mr. Victor

Akinpelu
Textbooks and other Materials:
⚫

Modern control theories: Non linear, optimal and Adaptive systems, Csaki.F (1972)

⚫

Applied Non linear control, Slotine, J.E (1991)

⚫

Control Theory: Multivariable and Non linear Methods, Khali, H

⚫

Non-linear Control systems, Marcel Dekker Inc, Vukic, Z, Kulija (2003)

a)

Course Overview and description

Non Linear Systems provides an advanced study of the principles, analysis, and applications of
nonlinear control systems, which are fundamental to modern engineering practice. Unlike linear
systems, nonlinear systems exhibit complex behaviors such as limit cycles, bifurcations, and
instability, making their analysis critical in real-world scenarios where ideal assumptions of
linearity no longer apply. The course introduces students to nonlinear differential equations,
common sources of nonlinearities in engineering systems, and approximation techniques
including linearization, piecewise linear methods, and describing functions for analyzing system
stability and predicting limit cycles. Students will gain practical skills in evaluating system
dynamics using phase-plane methods and Lyapunov’s stability theory, which are powerful tools
for assessing nonlinear responses and ensuring robust control. In addition, the course bridges
theory and practice by covering sampled-data systems with z-transform analysis and by
incorporating programmable logic controllers (PLCs) for process automation applications, thus
equipping learners with both analytical and industrially relevant competencies. Emphasis is
placed on developing the ability to model, analyze, and design nonlinear control strategies for
complex systems encountered in robotics, power systems, aerospace, and industrial automation.
By the end of the course, students will be able to formulate nonlinear system models, apply
appropriate approximation and stability techniques, and implement control logic for practical
applications. Overall, this course builds a solid foundation in nonlinear control, preparing
students for advanced research, professional practice, and innovation in electrical, electronic, and
control engineering fields.

l)

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments are designed to challenge students to think critically

and solve problems in the world of electromagnetism, analyze real-world scenarios and propose
solutions using the acquired knowledge of electromagnetic principles. The course also includes
assignments designed to solidify your understanding through calculations, problem-solving
exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving electromagnetic phenomena.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Nonlinear systems play a critical role in addressing modern engineering challenges where realworld processes rarely behave linearly. From renewable energy integration and smart grids to
robotics, aerospace, and autonomous vehicles, nonlinear dynamics govern system behavior and
stability. Contemporary issues such as sustainability, efficiency, and safety demand advanced
nonlinear modeling, analysis, and control strategies. Industrially, the course equips students with
tools like phase-plane methods, Lyapunov stability, and PLC-based automation, directly relevant
to manufacturing, power systems, and process industries. Mastery of nonlinear system analysis
prepares engineers to innovate and solve complex, evolving problems in modern technologydriven industries.
