a)

Course Overview and description

This course provides a solid foundation in power system engineering, covering the structure and
operation of electrical networks. Topics include power stations and their roles, economic
operation, and power factor correction. Students will study polyphase theory, voltage profiles in
D.C. and A.C. distribution, and transmission line characteristics such as inductance, capacitance,
skin effect, corona discharge, and bundled conductors. The course also examines transmission
materials and introduces load forecasting methods for effective planning and reliability.

Group works and Assignments
The group assignments in this course are designed to translate power system theory into

practical competency. Through problem-based learning (PBL), students will analyze real-world
power system scenarios—such as economic dispatch, voltage profile management, and
transmission line design—and propose viable engineering solutions. These activities are
structured to solidify understanding of core principles, including polyphase systems, power
factor correction, and electromagnetic phenomena like skin and corona effects, thereby achieving
the course's learning outcomes.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
This course equips students with the essential skills to address modern challenges in the

evolving electrical power industry. It focuses on the analysis of power systems with a touch on
economic operation. Key topics include transmission line analytic calculations, load forecasting
in power systems, and industrial power factor correction for cost and carbon reduction.
Ultimately, the course prepares students to contribute to developing reliable, economical, and
sustainable power systems for the future.

a)

Course Overview and description

Digital Systems and Electronics provides students with a comprehensive understanding of the
principles and applications of modern instrumentation, sensors, and electronic systems used in
measurement and industrial automation. The course introduces the fundamental concepts of
instrumentation science, focusing on the role of sensors and transducers in capturing various
physical parameters such as temperature, pressure, force, light, and flow. Students learn signal
processing and interfacing techniques to improve data accuracy, reduce noise, and ensure
effective communication between hardware and software components. Emphasis is placed on
digital logic, microcontroller programming, and system integration, enabling learners to design
and implement data acquisition and control systems. The course also explores noise reduction
strategies, logic gate operations, and the application of expert system instrumentation, preparing
students for real-world challenges in electronics and digital measurement. By analyzing
industrial case studies, learners gain practical insights into how instrumentation supports
efficiency, safety, and innovation in diverse engineering fields such as manufacturing, healthcare,
and automation. With a balance of theoretical principles and hands-on application, EEE 471
equips students with the analytical, technical, and problem-solving skills necessary to design,
evaluate, and deploy effective digital instrumentation solutions. Ultimately, the course not only
builds proficiency in modern electronic systems but also fosters adaptability to evolving

128

technologies, bridging the gap between academic knowledge and contemporary industrial
practices.

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments are designed to challenge students to think critically

and solve problems in the world of electromagnetism, analyze real-world scenarios and propose
solutions using the acquired knowledge of electromagnetic principles. The course also includes
assignments designed to solidify your understanding through calculations, problem-solving
exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving electromagnetic phenomena.

m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Digital Systems and Electronics is highly relevant in addressing contemporary challenges driven
by rapid technological advancements in automation, smart manufacturing, and data-driven
industries. With the growing demand for precision, efficiency, and reliability, modern systems
increasingly rely on advanced sensors, microcontrollers, and signal processing for accurate
measurement and control. The course equips students to tackle industrial issues such as noise
interference, system integration, and intelligent instrumentation, which are vital in sectors like
healthcare, energy, and manufacturing. Its focus on digital and expert systems also aligns with
Industry 4.0, IoT applications, and the push toward sustainable and innovative industrial
solutions.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)
College of Engineering
Department of Electrical/Electronics and Computer Engineering
Course Code: EEE 502

Course Title: Electrical Design and Services

Semester: Second

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Hall B2-3

Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M.O Onibonoje, Mr. Victor

Akinpelu
Textbooks and other Materials:
- A textbook of Electrical Technology Volume I-III by B.L and A.K Theraja (2005)
-Handbook of Electrical Design Details Neil Sclater and John E.Traister (2003)
-Design of Electrical Services for Buildings Fourth Edition Barrie Rigby (2005)
a)

Course Overview and description

This advanced-level course provides a comprehensive exploration of the principles and practices
involved in the design and implementation of electrical services in residential, commercial, and
industrial buildings. The course emphasizes both theoretical foundations and practical design
considerations essential for professional electrical engineers. Key topics include the design and
installation of lighting and power systems, analysis of energy supply and distribution networks,
and the selection of appropriate cables, wiring methods, and accessories to ensure efficient and
safe operation. Students will examine outdoor low voltage lines and cables, with a focus on
technical and environmental challenges in overhead and underground installations. The course
also covers protection systems and their characteristics, equipping students with the skills to
design protective schemes that comply with safety standards and operational reliability.
Emphasis is placed on proper earthing methods and the testing of electrical installations to ensure
compliance with national and international codes. An in-depth study of illumination principles
complements the course, enabling students to design effective lighting systems based on factors
such as lux levels, uniformity, energy efficiency, and user comfort. By the end of the course,
students will be capable of producing detailed electrical service designs that are compliant,
efficient, and tailored to specific building requirements, preparing them for roles in consultancy,
construction, or further specialization in electrical systems engineering.

a)

Course Overview and description

This course covers the fundamental concepts of Finite State Machine: definition, mealy and
Moore models, state diagram, state table, transition table. Sequential circuits design using
flip-flops, asynchronous, and synchronous circuit design. Algorithm State Machine. Design
examples and exercises. Structured Design: Design constructs, Design Levels, Geometrybased interchange formats, Computer aided electronic system design tools, Schematic
circuit capture, Hardware description languages, Design process (simulation, synthesis),
Structural design decomposition. Introduction to VHDL: VHDL language abstractions,
Design hierarchies, VHDL component, Lexical description, VHDL source file, Data types,
Data objects, Language statements, Concurrent VHDL, Sequential VHDL, Advanced
features of VHDL (library, package and subprograms). Structural level modelling, RegisterTransfer level modelling, FSM with datapath level modelling, Algorithmic level modelling.
Introduction of ASIC, Types of ASIC, ASIC design process, Standard cell ASIC synthesis,
FPGA Design Paradigm, FPGA synthesis, FPGA/CPLD Architectures. VHDL Design: Topdown design flow, Verification, simulation alternatives, simulation speed, Formal
verification, Recommendations for verification, Writing RTL VHDL code for synthesis, topdown design with FPGA. VHDL synthesis, optimization and mapping, constraints,
technology library, delay calculation, synthesis tool, synthesis directives. Computer-aided
design of logic circuits.

EEE 511

Course Title:

Digital Signal Processing

Semester:

First

Credit Hours:

3

Contact Hours:

Lecture - Two hours

Location:

B2-3

lectures per Week
Lecturer(s):

Textbooks and other Materials:
1. S. Esakkirajan , T. Veerakumar, Badri N Subudhi, Digital Signal Processing, Springer
Singapore, XVIII, 523p, ISBN: 978-981-99-6751-3. DOI: 10.1007/978-981-99-6752-0
2. Maurice Bellanger; Benjamin A. Engel, Digital Signal Processing: Theory and Practice,
Published by IEEE, 370p.
a)

Course Overview and description

This course discusses the advantages of digital over analogue signal processing, highlights the
problems of digitization, and presents an overview of the applications of digital signal processing
(DSP), basic elements of DSP system. In addition, the course covers the fundamental concepts of
digital processing of analogue signals: Sampling of analogue signals, sampling theorem, aliasing,
quantization, noise, and coding, types and selection of ADC/DAC, Sigma-delta ADC. Furthermore,
analytical tools such as z-transform, properties, transfer function, inverse z-transform, z-plane poles
and zeros, analysis of linear time-invariant in z-domain, system stability are studied. The concept of
discrete Fourier analysis and its various categories are studied. Lastly, the digital filter definition
150

and types are presented.

Term Paper and Assignments

m)

A term paper focus on comparative analysis of digital and analog signal processing in
modern applications. This will focus on the advantages, limitations, and relevance in areas
like communications, imaging, and healthcare.
Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

The course equips students with the knowledge to address critical challenges digital filter design,
conversion of analog signals to digital. Contemporary issues in Digital Signal Processing (DSP) are
shaped by the rapid growth of data-driven technologies, the need for real-time processing, and the
demand for energy-efficient systems in modern devices. With the proliferation of IoT, 5G/6G
153

networks, and multimedia applications, DSP must address challenges such as quantization noise,
aliasing, and digitization errors while maintaining high accuracy and speed. Emerging trends also
highlight the integration of DSP with artificial intelligence and machine learning for advanced
pattern recognition and adaptive filtering, while cybersecurity and ethical concerns around signal
manipulation, surveillance, and privacy further emphasize the need for robust and secure DSP
techniques. Additionally, the evolution of specialized hardware platforms such as GPUs, FPGAs,
and dedicated DSP processors underscores the importance of balancing computational efficiency
with

flexibility

in

implementation.

The

industrial

relevance

of

DSP

spans

across

telecommunications, consumer electronics, healthcare, automotive systems, defense, and
entertainment. In telecommunications, DSP enables reliable data transmission through modulation,
compression, and error correction, while in healthcare it underpins diagnostic imaging, biomedical
signal analysis, and hearing aids. Modern automobiles and autonomous systems rely on DSP for
radar, LiDAR, and real-time sensor fusion, whereas defense applications use it for secure
communication, radar, and sonar systems. Consumer technologies such as smartphones, digital
cameras, and streaming platforms also depend heavily on DSP for noise reduction, image
enhancement, and data compression. By providing the analytical and practical tools to process,
analyze, and interpret signals effectively, DSP remains central to advancing innovations in multiple
industries and addressing the technological needs of a data-driven world.

Power Systems

Textbooks and other Materials:

McGraw Hill Education Private Ltd.
2. C. L. Wadhwa (2009), Electrical Power Systems, 4th Edition, New Age International
Ltd.
3. J. B. Gupta, (2021), A Course in Power Systems, 11th Edition, S k Kataria & Sons
4. Journal and Conference Papers.
a)

Course Overview and description

This course provides a comprehensive exploration of electric power systems, focusing on the
theoretical and practical aspects of transmission line performance through Kirchhoff’s laws and
Ohm’s law. Students will analyze power generation, voltage control mechanisms, and the
phenomena of voltage collapse within large-scale grids. The curriculum delves into the
formulation of mathematical models for power system stability and the interpretation of system
responses under various disturbances. It also covers the principles and justification for using
power line carrier systems for high-frequency communication. Finally, the course critically
assesses the impact of deregulation, smart grid, and micro-grid technologies on the Nigerian
power sector, emphasizing the integration of distributed generation for enhanced reliability and
efficiency.

l)

Group works and Assignments
Group work and assignments in this course are designed to reinforce the core learning

outcomes by encouraging collaborative analysis and practical application. Students work in
teams to evaluate transmission line performance using Kirchhoff’s laws and Ohm’s law, simulate
power system stability under disturbances, and model voltage control scenarios. Assignments
include case studies on voltage collapse, mathematical formulation of stability equations, and the
use of power line carrier systems for communication. Groups also assess the impact of
deregulation and smart grid technologies on Nigeria’s power sector, proposing integration
strategies for distributed generation. This approach fosters critical thinking, teamwork, and realworld problem-solving.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
Contemporary issues and industrial relevance are integrated into the course through

targeted group projects and assignments that reflect real-world challenges in the power sector.
Students critically examine voltage collapse incidents, power system disturbances, and the role
of high-frequency communication technologies in modern grid operations. Assignments also
explore the impact of deregulation, smart grid innovations, and distributed generation on the
Nigerian electricity market, fostering awareness of evolving industry practices. By engaging with
current trends and technologies, students develop practical insights and problem-solving skills
aligned with the demands of today’s power industry.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)

Course Title:

Power System Engineering

Semester:

First

Credit Hours:

3

Lecture - Three hours

lectures per Week
Lecturer(s):

Engr. Prof. O. J. Dada/Dr. Omogbai

159

Textbooks and other Materials:
1. D. P. Kothari & I. J. Nagarath, Modern Power System Analysis
2. J. B. Gupta, A Course in Power Systems.
3. Electrical Power Systems - C. L. Wadhwa
a)

Course Overview and description

This course provides a comprehensive foundation in power system analysis, equipping students
to model, analyze, and solve key operational challenges. Students will learn to represent complex
power networks using one-line diagrams and per-unit systems, leading to the application of load
flow methods like Gauss-Seidel and Newton-Raphson to determine steady-state voltages and
power flows. The curriculum extends to both symmetrical and unsymmetrical fault analysis,
enabling the calculation of fault currents, the design of mitigation strategies, and the modeling of
sequence networks. Finally, the course covers the fundamental principles of power system
protection, including fault clearing sequences and discrimination methods, ensuring a holistic
understanding of system reliability and security.

Group works and Assignments
Group assignments in this course is emphasized through problem-based learning (PBL)

to apply power system analysis and protection concepts. Students will use one-line diagrams,
per-unit analysis, load flow methods, and fault studies to evaluate system conditions, design
mitigation strategies, and analyze protection schemes, thereby reinforcing the course learning
outcomes.

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
This course develops practical skills in modern power system analysis and protection,

addressing industrial needs for reliability, security, and efficiency. Students learn to apply load
flow methods, fault analysis, and protection principles to real-world challenges such as grid
stability, equipment protection, and sustainable system operation. By integrating theoretical tools
with industry-relevant practices, the course prepares graduates to tackle contemporary issues in
power system planning and operation.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)

Course Code: EEE 561

Course Title: Control Engineering

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Two hours lectures per week
Location: Hall B-33

Lecturer(s): Engr. Prof. M.O Onibonoje, Mr. Victor

Akinpelu
166

Textbooks and other Materials:

· Linear State-Space Control Systems – Robert L. Williams
· Modern Control Engineering – Katsuhiko Ogata

a)

Course Overview and description

Control Engineering provides students with an advanced understanding of modern control
theory through the state-space approach, equipping them with both theoretical foundations and
practical skills for the analysis and design of dynamic systems. The course begins with the
formulation of state-space models and their relationship to transfer functions, enabling learners
to bridge classical and modern control representations. Students will explore the role of
eigenvalues and eigenvectors in determining system response and stability, while also mastering
critical concepts such as controllability and observability that form the backbone of system
design. Building on this foundation, the course emphasizes the design and implementation of
state feedback controllers, observers, and compensators, with a strong focus on optimal control
through the Linear Quadratic Regulator (LQR) framework. Real-world applications, including
robotics, circuit synthesis, and signal processing, are integrated to illustrate the industrial
relevance of control strategies. Additionally, students will gain hands-on experience with
computer-aided tools for simulation, modeling, and optimization, preparing them for
contemporary engineering challenges. By the end of the course, learners are expected to
demonstrate the ability to analyze, design, and optimize control systems that meet performance
criteria under practical constraints, while also appreciating the interdisciplinary applications of
control engineering across electrical, mechanical, and electronic domains. This course thus fosters
problem-solving, innovation, and lifelong learning, while strengthening students’ readiness to
contribute effectively in research, industry, and advanced studies.
b) Pre-requisites: Nil
c) Co-requisite(s): Nil
d) Role in Curriculum: Core

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments are designed to challenge students to think critically

and solve problems in the world of electromagnetism, analyze real-world scenarios and propose
solutions using the acquired knowledge of electromagnetic principles. The course also includes
assignments designed to solidify your understanding through calculations, problem-solving
exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving electromagnetic phenomena.

m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Control Engineering remains at the forefront of contemporary technological advancement,
addressing critical issues such as automation, system optimization, and energy efficiency in
modern industries. With the increasing reliance on robotics, autonomous systems, and intelligent
manufacturing, mastery of state-space methods and optimal control strategies equips engineers
to design reliable and adaptable solutions. In fields like aerospace, automotive, power systems,
and signal processing, control techniques ensure safety, stability, and performance under
complex constraints. The course’s emphasis on computer-aided design tools further aligns with
industry demands for digitalization, smart technologies, and innovative problem-solving in
rapidly evolving engineering environments..

Course Code: EEE 513

Course Title: Communication Systems Engineering

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Three hours lectures per week
Location: Hall B2-5

Lecturer(s): Engr. Dr. A. O. Ojo & Engr. A. Akinola

Textbooks and other Materials:
- Owolabi & Ojo (2022), Electrical Communication Principles, Systems and Applications, 1st
Edition, Matprints Inc.
- Marcelo & Valdemar (2005), Communication Systems, 3rd Edition, Springer.

a)

Course Overview and description

This course provides a comprehensive introduction to the principles and applications of modern
communication systems, focusing on both foundational and advanced topics. It covers signal
representation, Fourier analysis, and the distinction between analog and digital signals, laying
the groundwork for understanding modulation techniques like AM, FM, and digital schemes
such as BASK and M-ary modulation. The course explores wireless communication, including
cellular systems, smart antennas, and the internet's role in global connectivity. The concluding
part of this course covers topics that include electromagnetic wave propagation, radar systems,
and satellite communication, with emphasis on real-world challenges like atmospheric effects.
Emerging technologies such as software-defined radio (SDR) and cognitive radio (CR) are
highlighted, focusing on their roles in dynamic spectrum management and efficient spectrum
utilization.
b)

Co-requisite(s): Nil

Group works and Assignments

The group works and assignments are designed to challenge students to think critically
and solve problems in the world of communication, analyze real-world scenarios and propose
solutions using the acquired knowledge of communication principles and system. The course also
includes assignments designed to solidify your understanding through calculations, problemsolving exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments involving modern communication
systems.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

The communication course addresses issues like spectrum scarcity, cybersecurity, 5G
rollout, energy efficiency, and IoT integration in smart cities. It is relevant to industries such as
telecommunications, satellite communication, radar systems, and consumer electronics, focusing
on technologies like SDR, CR, signal modulation, channel coding, and electromagnetic wave
propagation. The course also covers spectrum regulation, mobile switching centers, and cellular
systems, all critical for advancing communication networks and addressing global challenges.

Course Title: Electric Circuit Theory II

Course Content or Outline

Topics

Weeks

Advanced Network Analysis Techniques
• Node and loop analysis methods for complex circuits
• Matrix formulation and solution of network equations
• Application of network theorems in multi-loop systems
Network Functions and Transfer Function Realization
• Definition and properties of network functions
• Realization of transfer functions using passive and active
components
• Foster and Cauer forms of network synthesis
Filter Design and Analysis
• Classification and characteristics of filters: low-pass, high-pass,
band-pass
• Butterworth and Chebyshev filter design principles
• Frequency response and bandwidth considerations
Non-Linear Circuit Analysis and Locus Diagrams
• Behavior of non-linear components in circuits
• Graphical analysis using locus diagrams
• Stability and response of non-linear networks
Active Network Synthesis and Applications
• Design of active networks using operational amplifiers
• Realization of complex transfer functions
• Practical considerations in active filter implementation
Advanced Filter Structures and Optimization
• M-derived and constant-k filters
• Filter cascading and impedance matching
• Design for minimal distortion and optimal power handling

Tutorials

l)

Group works and Assignments
The group works and assignments in Circuit Theory II are designed to challenge students

to think critically and solve complex problems in electrical network analysis and synthesis.
Students will engage with real-world circuit scenarios, applying advanced concepts such as
transfer function realization, filter design, and non-linear analysis to propose effective
engineering solutions. The course also includes assignments aimed at reinforcing understanding
through rigorous calculations, analytical exercises, and potentially, simulations or experiments
involving modern circuit configurations and active network synthesis.
m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
Circuit Theory II addresses modern challenges such as the need for high-performance

filter design, efficient network synthesis, and the analysis of non-linear systems in increasingly
complex electrical environments. It also engages with emerging demands in signal integrity,
active circuit optimization, and the integration of analog-digital hybrid systems in smart
technologies. This course is highly relevant to industries such as power electronics, embedded
systems, telecommunications hardware, and control engineering, where precise circuit behavior
and network design are critical. It supports technologies like active filter implementation, transfer
function realization, and operational amplifier-based systems, all essential for developing
reliable, scalable, and efficient electronic solutions in modern industrial applications.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)

Department of Electrical/Electronics and Computer Engineering
Course Code: EEE 316

Course Title: Electromagnetic Field and Waves II

Semester: Second

Credit Hours: 3 hour

Contact Hours: Lecture - Three hours lecture per week
Location: Hall B2-5

- Owolabi I. E. and Ojo A. O., Electromagnetic Field and Waves, Principles and Applications,
Matprints Nigeria.
- Kraus, T. D., Electromagnetics, 3rd edition, McGraw Hill, New York.
a)

Course Overview and description

This course provides an in-depth exploration of electromagnetic theory, focusing on Maxwell’s
equations, their derivations, and applications in wave propagation, transmission lines,
waveguides, and antennae. Students will learn to analyze electromagnetic phenomena using
vector calculus, study wave behavior in various media (conductors, ionized media, and across
boundaries), and apply wave theory to communication systems. Practical exercises and
simulations will reinforce theoretical concepts, preparing students for advanced topics in
electrical and communication engineering.

k)

Tutorials
Tutorial will be offered at the end of each module.

l)

Group works and Assignments

The group works and assignments in Electromagnetic Field and Waves II are crafted to
deepen students’ analytical skills and foster mastery in solving complex electromagnetic field
problems. Learners will engage with real-world scenarios involving wave propagation, boundary
interactions, and electromagnetic system design. Through rigorous tasks, students will derive
and interpret Maxwell’s equations across coordinate systems, apply scalar and vector potentials
to field modeling, and analyze wave behavior using boundary conditions and the Poynting
vector.
Assignments will challenge students to evaluate phenomena such as skin effect and plasma
frequency in conductors and ionized media, assessing their impact on signal transmission.
Practical components may include simulations or experiments involving transmission lines,
waveguides, and antenna systems, reinforcing theoretical understanding through hands-on
exploration. These activities are designed to cultivate critical thinking, precision in mathematical
modeling, and the ability to translate electromagnetic theory into effective engineering solutions.

192

m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Electromagnetic Field and Waves II addresses contemporary challenges in the modeling, analysis,
and application of electromagnetic fields across complex environments. The course equips
students with the ability to derive and interpret Maxwell’s equations in multiple coordinate
systems, apply scalar and vector potentials, and analyze wave behavior at media interfaces using
boundary conditions and energy flow principles.
It responds to emerging demands in high-frequency communication, electromagnetic
compatibility, and the design of efficient transmission systems. Students engage with advanced
topics such as wave propagation in conductors and ionized media, antenna theory, and the
optimization of waveguides and transmission lines for modern communication networks. This
course is highly relevant to industries including wireless communications, radar systems, satellite
technology, and RF engineering, where precise electromagnetic modeling and system design are
essential.
Technologies supported include antenna array design, impedance matching, energy-efficient
waveguide systems, and electromagnetic simulation tools—critical for developing scalable, highperformance solutions in today’s interconnected and signal-sensitive world.

Course Code: EEE 564
Course Title: Seminars on the Use of Software for Electrical Electronic Engineering
Semester: Second

Press.
- Moore, H. (2018), MATLAB for Engineers, 5th Edition, Pearson.

193

a)

Course Overview and description

This seminar course introduces students to the application of software tools in solving real-world
problems in electrical and electronics engineering. It covers software for circuit design,
simulation, analysis, control systems, and project management. Through lectures, hands-on
sessions, case studies, and student-led seminars, participants will explore industry-standard
tools, their applications, and emerging trends. Students will develop skills in selecting and using
appropriate software for engineering tasks and present a seminar on a chosen software tool.

l) Group Works and Assignments
The group works and assignments in Seminars on the Use of Software for Electrical Electronic
Engineering are designed to cultivate students’ proficiency in applying digital tools to solve
practical engineering problems. Learners will engage with real-world scenarios involving circuit
design, control systems, and signal analysis, using industry-standard software platforms to
simulate, optimize, and evaluate system performance.
Assignments will challenge students to identify appropriate software tools for specific
engineering tasks, compare their features and limitations, and implement basic electrical or
electronic systems through simulation. Group projects may include collaborative design
exercises, comparative tool evaluations, and seminar presentations that integrate technical
analysis with practical demonstrations. These activities are structured to enhance critical
thinking, digital fluency, and the ability to translate software capabilities into effective
engineering solutions.
m) Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
Seminars on the Use of Software for Electrical Electronic Engineering addresses the growing
importance of software-driven design and analysis in modern electrical engineering practice. The
course responds to contemporary challenges such as the integration of analog-digital hybrid
systems, signal integrity in high-speed circuits, and the need for efficient simulation in complex
embedded environments.
Students explore current trends in software applications for power systems, control engineering,
and telecommunications hardware, gaining insight into how digital tools support innovation in
smart technologies and automation. The course is highly relevant to industries such as embedded

196

systems, power electronics, industrial automation, and electronic product development, where
software proficiency is essential for reliability, scalability, and performance.
Technologies supported include circuit simulation platforms, control system modeling tools, and
power system analysis software which are critical for developing intelligent, adaptive, and
efficient engineering solutions in today’s digitally transformed landscape.

Course Title: Numerical Computations
Credit
3

a)

Course Overview and description

This course covers the fundamental concepts of numerical analysis with specific
emphasis on the various numerical methods and tools essential for analyzing and solving
engineering problems with minimal errors. Topics include errors in numerical analysis,
convergence theory of computational solution to problem: numerical algorithm
formulation and design, numerical software systems. Iterative solutions of non-linear
systems: (Newton’s Method). Numerical solutions of linear systems. Numerical
computation of Eigen values and Eigen vectors. Curve fitting; function approximation.
197

Numerical differentiation and integration (Simpson’s Rule etc.). Explicit and implicit
methods. Differential equation (Euler’s method etc.). Numerical integration and
differentiation. Linear algebra. Finite differences. Introduction to the use of Octave,
MATLAB or LabVIEW in numerical computation and engineering applications.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)
College of Engineering
Department of Electrical/Electronics and Computer Engineering
Course Code: EEE 427

Course Title: Data Communication and Networks

Semester: First

Credit Hours: 3 hours

Contact Hours: Lecture - Three hours lectures per week
Location: Hall B2-5

Lecturer(s): Engr. Dr. A. O. Ojo & Engr. Olawale

Textbooks and other Materials:
- Owolabi & Ojo (2022), Electrical Communication Principles, Systems and Applications, 1st
Edition, Matprints Inc.
- Prakash C. Gupta, (2014), Data Communication and Computer Networks, 2nd Edition, PHI
Learning Private Limited.

a)

Course Overview and description

Introduction to Data communications: the Development of Data Communications; types and
sources of data, simple communications network, transmission definitions, one way
transmission, half duplex transmission, transmission codes, transmission modes, parallel
transmission, serial transmission, bit synchronization, character synchronization, character
202

synchronization,

synchronous

transmission,

asynchronous

transmission,

efficiency

of

transmission, error detection methods and data compression. Protocols: Introduction to network
protocol. Seven Layer ISO-OSI standard protocols and network architecture. Transport protocols,
session services protocols, and other protocols. Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineering
802 standards. Error control and Data Compression: Forward Error Control; error detection
methods; parity checking; linear block codes, cyclic redundancy checking; feedback error control,
data compression, Huffman coding and dynamic Huffman coding. Local Area Networks:
medium access control techniques – Ethernet, token bus and token ring; LAN standards; fibre
distributed data interface, metropolitan area network. Peer-to-peer, Client Server. Client-Server
Requirements: GUI design standards, interface independence, platform independence,
transaction processing, and connectivity, reliability, backup and recovery mechanisms.
Information Network Software; Features and benefits of major recovery mechanisms.
Information Network Software: features and benefits of major Network Operating Systems.
Network OS: (e.g. Novell NetWare, UNIX/LINUX, OS/2 & Windows NT). TCP/IP and Network
OS. INTERNET: Definition, architecture, services, Internet addressing. Internet protocol, IPv4,
IPv6. Internet programming, Intranet. System administration, and security issues.
b)

Pre-requisites: Nil

c)

Co-requisite(s): Nil

d)

k)

Tutorials
Tutorial will be offered at the end of each module.

l)

Group works and Assignments

The group works and assignments in EEE 427: Data Communication and Networks are designed to
build students’ technical fluency and collaborative problem-solving skills in digital

208

communication systems. Learners will work on real-world scenarios such as designing LAN
topologies, configuring network protocols, and simulating data transmission models.
Assignments will include protocol analysis, error control coding exercises, and comparative
evaluations of network operating systems. Group projects may involve building client-server
models, simulating packet transmission, and presenting case studies on Internet architecture and
security. These activities foster analytical thinking, teamwork, and the ability to apply theoretical
knowledge to practical networking challenges.

m)

Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance

Data Communication and Networks addresses the evolving landscape of digital communication,
where speed, reliability, and security are paramount. The course responds to contemporary
demands in areas such as high-speed data transmission, secure Internet protocols, and scalable
network architectures.
Students explore current trends in IPv6 deployment, cloud-based networking, and cybersecurity
frameworks. The course is highly relevant to industries including telecommunications, enterprise
IT, embedded systems, and Internet infrastructure—where robust data communication is
foundational.
Technologies supported include TCP/IP stack implementation, LAN simulation tools, network
OS platforms (e.g., Linux, Windows Server), and Internet protocol analysis—critical for
developing resilient, efficient, and secure communication systems in today’s connected world.

AFE BABALOLA UNIVERSITY ADO-EKITI (ABUAD)
College of Engineering
Department of Electrical/Electronics and Computer Engineering
Course Code:

EEE 535

Course Title:

High

Frequency

and

Microwave Electronics
Semester:

First

Contact Hours:

Lecture

Credit Hours:
-

Three

hours Location:

3
B2-1

lectures per Week
Lecturer(s):

Engr. T. E. Fabunmi

Textbooks and other Materials
Pozar, D. M. (2012). Microwave Engineering. Wiley, 4th Edition.
Collin, R. E. (2001). Foundations for Microwave Engineering. Wiley-IEEE Press.
Gupta, K. C., & Verma, R. (2014). Microwave Engineering. S. Chand Publications.
Liao, S. Y. (2010). Microwave Devices and Circuits. Pearson, 3rd Edition.
Lecture notes, PowerPoint slides
a) Course Overview and Description
This course surveys high-frequency and microwave electronics. It introduces wave propagation
in waveguides and resonators, passive and active components, and modern semiconductor
microwave devices. Students will learn about directional couplers, isolators, circulators,
klystrons, magnetrons, traveling-wave tubes, parametric amplifiers, and solid-state microwave
sources such as Gunn diodes, PIN diodes, and varactors. The course also explores optoelectronic
devices such as photodiodes and phototransistors and their applications in communication and
radar systems.

l) Term Paper and Assignments

simulation and design tasks on passive and active components.
m) Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
Microwave electronics is vital for modern telecommunications, radar, satellite communication,
and defense systems. This course equips students with knowledge relevant to 5G/6G wireless
technologies, IoT connectivity, and aerospace applications. Graduates with this training are
highly valued in industries developing sustainable and high-speed communication systems.

Lecture notes, PowerPoint slides
a) Course Overview and Description
This course provides a foundation in feedback control theory and servomechanisms. Students
will study system modeling (electrical, mechanical, hydraulic, pneumatic, and thermal),
frequency response techniques, stability analysis, compensation methods, and controller design.
The course integrates practical servomechanism applications with theoretical analysis to prepare
students for modern control engineering challenges in industry.

235

l) Term Paper and Assignments
Students will submit a term paper analyzing a real-world servomechanism (e.g., robotics,
aerospace control, automotive control). Assignments will include transfer function modeling,
stability analysis, and compensator design problems.
m) Contemporary Issues and Industrial Relevance
This course prepares students for careers in automation, robotics, aerospace, and power systems.
Servomechanism applications are key in robotics, renewable energy, automotive systems (ABS,
cruise control), and process industries. By learning modern control methods, students gain skills
directly relevant to emerging technologies and sustainable automation.

236

Class notes, PowerPoint slides, and online resources provided by the lecturer.
a) Course Overview and Description
This course introduces students to the fundamental principles and applications of electric drives.
It covers various motor types, speed–torque characteristics, speed control techniques, braking,
motor rating and selection, protection, and control. Emphasis is placed on the industrial and
commercial applications of electric drives, their role in automation, and their relevance in modern
engineering practice.

c) Co-requisite(s): Nil

a)

Course Overview and description

This course covers the operations and applications operational amplifiers (Op-amp) for linear and
non-linear operations, dynamic logic systems and digital circuits in instrumentation. Topics include;
Linear I.C. op-amp, linear and non-linear operations, logarithmic amplifiers, Analog-to-Digital
(ADC) and Digital-to- Analog (DAC) converters, gyrators and negative impedance converters, the
555-timer structure and applications; four-quadrant multipliers, dynamic logic systems and RAM
memory circuits.

SECTION FIVE: CURRICULUM AND LEARNING
PROCESS

245

The Curriculum
The Curriculum
Discuss the program structure and course contents to show how they are appropriate to,
consistent with, and support the development of the range of intellectual and practical skills
and attainment or achievement of the PLOs:
The program structure and course contents are designed to be appropriate, consistent, and
supportive of developing a range of intellectual and practical skills, ensuring the attainment of
the Programme Learning Outcomes (PLOs). The curriculum is carefully crafted to provide a
comprehensive education in Electrical/Electronic Engineering, integrating both theoretical
knowledge and hands-on experience. Each course is aligned with the PLOs, contributing to the
overall educational objectives of the programme. Core courses cover fundamental concepts,
advanced theories, and practical applications, ensuring that students gain a deep understanding
of the field. Elective courses allow students to specialize in areas of interest, further enhancing
their expertise and skills. Laboratory work, projects, and internships are integral components of
the programme, providing students with opportunities to apply their knowledge in real-world
scenarios. These practical experiences are essential for developing problem-solving abilities,
technical proficiency, and professional competencies. The curriculum includes courses on ethics,
communication, and management to ensure that graduates are well-rounded professionals
capable of addressing complex challenges in their careers. The programme structure and course
contents are meticulously designed to support the holistic development of students, equipping
them with the necessary skills and knowledge to achieve the PLOs and excel in the field of
Electrical/Electronic Engineering.
Distribution of the Engineering Courses
Visit departmental handbook for details on the course structure and course contents at
departmental website

i. Compulsory Courses: Students must register for and pass before graduation.
ii. Elective Courses: Courses students may offer and pass to obtain the minimum units required
for graduation. Such courses are normally selected from within or outside the programme with
the approval of the department.
iii. Required Course: A course specified by a programme which a student shall take and pass.
247

iv. Pre-requisite Courses: Courses students must take and pass before taking another specified
course.
v. Institutional Courses: Courses whose knowledge is essential for the preparation of Afe
Babalola University for future employment after graduation.
vi. Carry-over Courses: On the account of any failure by a student to satisfy a pass requirement
in any course, such student will be required to repeat only the course/courses the following
academic session or re-write the prescribed examination in such course at the end of the academic
session.
Students carrying over courses shall not be allowed to register for more than the approved
maximum number of credits for the semester.

5.1. Course Description
100 LEVEL
GST 111: Communication in English (2 Units C: LH=15; PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, students should be able to:
1. identify possible sound patterns in English Language;
2. list notable language skills;
3. classify word formation processes;
4. construct simple and fairly complex sentences in English;
5. apply logical and critical reasoning skills for meaningful presentations;
6. demonstrate an appreciable level of the art of public speaking and listening; and
7. write simple and technical reports.
Course Contents
Sounds and sound patterns in English Language (vowels and consonants, phonetics and
phonology). English word classes (lexical and grammatical words, definitions, forms, functions,
usages, collocations). major word formation processes; the sentence in English (types: structural
and functional). grammar and usage (tense, concord and modality). Reading and types of
reading, comprehension skills, 3RsQ. Logical and critical thinking; reasoning methods (logic and
syllogism, inductive and deductive argument, analogy, generalisation and explanations). Ethical
considerations, copyright rules and infringements. Writing activities: pre-writing (brainstorming
and outlining). writing (paragraphing, punctuation and expression). post- writing (editing and
proofreading). Types of writing (summary, essays, letter, curriculum vitae, report writing, notemaking) etc. Mechanics of writing. Information and Communication Technology in modern
language learning. Language skills for effective communication. The art of public speaking.
GET 101: Engineer in Society (1 Unit C: LH=15)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. differentiate between science, engineering and technology, and relate them to innovation;
2. distinguish between the different cadres of engineering – engineers, technologists, technicians
and craftsmen and their respective roles and competencies;
3. identify and distinguish between the relevant professional bodies in engineering;
4. categorise the goals of global development or sustainable development goals (SDGs); and
254

5. identify and evaluate safety and risk in engineering practice.
Course Contents
History, evolution and philosophy of science. engineering and technology. The engineering
profession – engineering family (engineers, technologists, technicians and craftsmen),
professional bodies and societies. Engineers' code of conduct and ethics, and engineering literacy.
Sustainable development goals (SDGs), innovation, infrastructures and nation building economy, politics, business. Safety and risk analysis in engineering practice. Engineering
competency skills – curriculum overview, technical, soft and digital skills. Guest seminars and
invited lectures from different engineering professional associations.
MTH 101: Elementary Mathematics I (Algebra and Trigonometry)
(2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course students should be able to:
1. define and explain set, subset, union, intersection, complements, and demonstrate the use of
Venn diagrams;
2. solve quadratic equations;
3. solve trigonometric functions;
4. identify various types of numbers; and
5. solve some problems using binomial theorem.
Course Contents
Elementary set theory, subsets, union, intersection, complements, Venn diagrams. Real numbers,
integers, rational and irrational numbers. Mathematical induction, real sequences and series,
theory of quadratic equations, binomial theorem, complex numbers, algebra of complex numbers,
the argand diagram. De-Moiré’s theorem, nth roots of unity. Circular measure, trigonometric
functions of angles of any magnitude, addition and factor formulae.
PHY 101: General Physics I (Mechanics) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the students should be able to:
1. identify and deduce the physical quantities and their units;
2. differentiate between vectors and scalars;
3. describe and evaluate motion of systems on the basis of the fundamental laws of mechanics;
4. apply Newton’s laws to describe and solve simple problems of motion;

255

5. evaluate work, energy, velocity, momentum, acceleration, and torque of moving or rotating
objects;
6. explain and apply the principles of conservation of energy, linear and angular momentum;
7. describe the laws governing motion under gravity; and
8. explain motion under gravity and quantitatively determine behaviour of objects moving under
gravity.
Course Contents
Space and time; units and dimension, vectors and scalars, differentiation of vectors: displacement,
velocity and acceleration; kinematics; Newton’s laws of motion (inertial frames, impulse, force
and action at a distance, momentum conservation); relative motion; application of Newtonian
mechanics; equations of motion; conservation principles in physics, conservative forces,
conservation of linear momentum, kinetic energy and work, potential energy, system of particles,
centre of mass; rotational motion; torque, vector product, moment, rotation of coordinate axes
and angular momentum. Polar coordinates; conservation of angular momentum; circular motion;
moments of inertia, gyroscopes and precession; gravitation: Newton’s law of gravitation, Kepler’s
laws of planetary motion, gravitational potential energy, escape velocity, satellites motion and
orbits.
PHY 107: General Practical Physics I (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the student should be able to:
1. conduct measurements of some physical quantities;
2. make observations of events, collect and tabulate data;
3. identify and evaluate some common experimental errors;
4. plot and analyse graphs; and
5. draw conclusions from numerical and graphical analysis of data.
Course Contents
This introductory course emphasizes quantitative measurements. Experimental techniques. The
treatment of measurement errors. Graphical analysis. The experiments include studies of meters,
the oscilloscope, mechanical systems, electrical and mechanical resonant systems, light, heat,
viscosity, etc. (covered in PHY 101, 102, 103 and PHY 104). However, emphasis should be placed
on the basic physical techniques for observation, measurements, data collection, analysis, and
deduction.

256

EEE 102: Introduction to Electrical and Electronics Engineering (2 units C: LH=15)
Learning Outcomes
Students will be able to:
1. comprehend the duties and functions of an Electrical and Electronics Engineer (EEE);
2. state the requirements for the profession and career opportunities;
3. state the careers related to EEE; and
4. explain the future of EEE.
Course Contents
History of Electrical Engineering. Evolution of EEE. Duties of EE Engineers. Areas of
specialisation and work environment. Skill requirements (soft and hard). Qualities for EE
Engineers. Careers related to EEE. Typical course modules. Job outlook/opportunities for EE
Engineers. Future of EEE. Professional registration (NSE, COREN, IEEE, IET, etc.). Passive
components (R, L, C, transformers): descriptive features, including values and colour codes, uses
in electrical circuits.DC and AC signal parameters
ABUAD-CHM 101: General Chemistry I (2 Units C: LH=30)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is essential for students at our university as it builds a strong foundation in chemistry,
aligning with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 4 (Quality Education) and 9 (Industry,
Innovation, and Infrastructure). A deep understanding of chemical principles is crucial for
advancing in scientific research, environmental sustainability, and technological innovation. By
mastering the content, students will be equipped to contribute to sustainable industrial practices,
environmental conservation, and scientific advancements, directly impacting global challenges.
This course fosters critical thinking and problem-solving skills, preparing students to be leaders
in science, technology, and sustainability.
Overview
This course introduces the fundamental concepts of chemistry, including atoms, molecules,
elements, and compounds. It explores modern electronic theory, electronic configuration, and
periodicity, and examines molecular structures, chemical bonding, and stoichiometry. Students
will also study the kinetic theory of matter, thermochemistry, reaction rates, equilibrium, and
thermodynamics. Additionally, the course covers the properties of gases, acids, bases, redox

257

reactions, electrochemistry, and radioactivity, providing a comprehensive understanding of
essential chemical principles.
Objectives
1. understand and describe the basic structure of atoms, molecules, elements, and
compounds.
2. apply electronic configuration and periodicity to explain chemical properties.
3. analyze and predict the shapes and structures of molecules and solids.
4. solve chemical equations and apply principles of stoichiometry, thermodynamics, and
kinetics.
5. understand and apply concepts of redox reactions, electrochemistry, and radioactivity.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. define atom, molecules and chemical reactions;
2. discuss the modern electronic theory of atoms;
3. write electronic configurations of elements on the periodic table;
4. rationalise the trends of atomic radii, ionisation energies, electronegativity of the elements,
based on their position in the periodic table;
5. identify and balance oxidation–reduction equation and solve redox titration problems;
6. draw shapes of simple molecules and hybridised orbitals;
7. identify the characteristics of acids, bases and salts, and solve problems based on their
quantitative relationship;
8. apply the principles of equilibrium to aqueous systems using LeChatelier’s principle to predict
the effect of concentration, pressure and temperature changes on equilibrium mixtures;
9. analyse and perform calculations with the thermodynamic functions, enthalpy, entropy and
free energy; and
10. determine rates of reactions and its dependence on concentration, time and temperature.
Course Contents
Atoms, molecules, elements and compounds, and chemical reactions. Modern electronic theory
of atoms. Electronic configuration, periodicity and building up of the periodic table.
Hybridisation and shapes of simple molecules. Valence forces; Structure of solids. Chemical
equations and stoichiometry; chemical bonding and intermolecular forces, kinetic theory of
matter. Elementary thermochemistry; rates of reaction, equilibrium and thermodynamics. Acids,
bases and salts. Properties of gases. Redox reactions and introduction to electrochemistry.
Radioactivity.

258

ABUAD-CHM 107: General Practical Chemistry I (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Senate-approved relevance
This laboratory course is integral to our university's commitment to Quality Education (SDG 4)
and Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG 12). It provides hands-on experience in
fundamental chemical techniques, reinforcing theoretical knowledge from CHM 101. By
mastering these practical skills, students develop the ability to conduct accurate experiments,
analyze data, and present findings effectively—essential competencies for careers in science,
industry, and research. The course also promotes safe and sustainable laboratory practices,
preparing students to contribute to sustainable industrial processes and environmental
stewardship.
Overview
This laboratory course offers practical experience in key chemical techniques, complementing the
theoretical content of CHM 101. Students will engage in acid-base titrations, qualitative analysis,
redox reactions, and gravimetric analysis. Emphasis is placed on accurate data collection,
analysis, and presentation, fostering essential laboratory skills. The course prepares students to
apply these techniques in scientific research and industry, ensuring a comprehensive
understanding of experimental chemistry.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
1. perform acid-base titrations with precision and accuracy.
2. conduct qualitative and quantitative analysis of chemical substances.
3. understand and execute redox reactions and gravimetric analysis.
4. analyze and interpret experimental data effectively.
5. present laboratory findings clearly and professionally.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the general laboratory rules and safety procedures;
2. collect scientific data and correct carry out chemical experiments;
3. identify the basic glassware and equipment in the laboratory;
4. state the differences between primary and secondary standards;
5. perform redox titration;
6. record observations and measurements in the laboratory notebooks; and
7. analyse the data to arrive at scientific conclusions.

259

Course Contents
Laboratory experiments designed to reflect topics presented in courses CHM 101 and CHM 102.
These include acid-base titrations, qualitative analysis, redox reactions, gravimetric analysis, data
analysis and presentation.
GST 112: Nigerian Peoples and Cultures (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the completion of the course, the students are expected to be able:
1. analyse the historical foundation of Nigerian cultures and arts in pre-colonial times;
2. identify and list the major linguistic groups in Nigeria;
3. explain the gradual evolution of Nigeria as a political entity;
4. analyse the concepts of trade and economic self-reliance of Nigerian peoples in relation to
national development;
5. enumerate the challenges of the Nigerian state regarding nation building;
6. analyse the role of the Judiciary in upholding fundamental human rights
7. identify the acceptable norms and values of the major ethnic groups in Nigeria; and
8. list possible solutions to identifiable Nigerian environmental, moral and value problems.
Course Contents
Nigerian history, culture and art up to 1800 (Yoruba, Hausa and Igbo peoples and cultures;
peoples and cultures of the minority ethnic groups). Nigeria under colonial rule (advent of
colonial rule in Nigeria; colonial administration of Nigeria). Evolution of Nigeria as a political
unit (amalgamation of Nigeria in 1914; formation of political parties in Nigeria; nationalist
movement and struggle for independence). Nigeria and challenges of nation building (military
intervention in Nigerian politics; Nigerian Civil War). Concepts of trade and economics of selfreliance (indigenous trade and market system; indigenous apprenticeship system among
Nigerian peoples; trade, skill acquisition and self-reliance). Social justice and national
development (definition and classification of law); Judiciary and fundamental rights. Individuals,
norms and values (basic Nigerian norms and values, patterns of citizenship acquisition;
citizenship and civic responsibilities; indigenous languages, usage and development; negative
attitudes and conducts [Cultism, kidnapping and other related social vices]). Re-orientation,
moral and national values (The 3Rs – Reconstruction, Rehabilitation and Re-orientation; reorientation strategies: Operation Feed the Nation (OFN), Green Revolution, Austerity Measures,
War Against Indiscipline and Corruption (WAIC), Mass Mobilization for Self-Reliance, Social
Justice and Economic Recovery (MAMSER), National Orientation Agency (NOA). Current sociopolitical and cultural developments in Nigeria.

260

GET 102: Engineering Graphics and Solid Modelling I (2 Units C: LH=15; PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. have a good grasp of design thinking and be obsessed with the determination to apply such to
solving simple every day and also complex problems;
2. recognize the fundamental concepts of engineering drawing and graphics;
3. show skills to represent the world of engineering objects in actionable solid models, and put
such models in a form where they can be inputs for simulation and analyses;
4. analyse such models for strength and cost.
5. prepare the objects for modern production and manufacturing techniques of additive and
subtractive manufacturing;
6. recognise that engineering is multidisciplinary in the sense that mechanical, electrical and other
parts of physical structures are modelled in context as opposed to the analytical nature of the
courses they take;and
7. analyse and master the basics of mechanical and thermal loads in engineering systems.
Course Contents
Introduction to design thinking and engineering graphics. First and third angle orthogonal
projections. Isometric projections; sectioning, conventional practices, conic sections and
development. Freehand and guided sketching – pictorial and orthographic. Visualisation and
solid modelling in design, prototyping and product-making. User interfaces in concrete terms.
Design, drawing, animation, rendering and simulation work spaces. Sketching of 3D objects.
Viewports and sectioning to shop drawings in orthographic projections and perspectives.
Automated viewports. Sheet metal and surface modelling. Material selection and rendering. This
course will use latest professional design tools such as fusion 360, solid works, solid edge or
equivalent.
MTH 102: Elementary Mathematics II (Calculus) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course, students should be able to:
1. identify the types of rules in differentiation and integration;
2. recognise and understand the meaning of function of a real variable, graphs, limits and
continuity;
3. solve some applications of definite integrals in areas and volumes;
4. solve function of a real variable, plot relevant graphs, identify limits and idea of continuity;
5. identify the derivative as limit of rate of change;
6. identify techniques of differentiation and perform extreme curve sketching;

261

7. identify integration as an inverse of differentiation;
8. identify methods of integration and definite integrals; and
9. perform integration application to areas, volumes.
Course Contents
Functions of a real variable, graphs, limits and idea of continuity. The derivative, as limit of rate
of change. Techniques of differentiation, maxima and minima. Extreme curve sketching,
integration, definite integrals, reduction formulae, application to areas, volumes (including
approximate integration: Trapezium and Simpson's rule).
PHY 102: General Physics II (Behaviour of Matter) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the students should be able to:
1. explain the concepts of heat and temperature and relate the temperature scales;
2. define, derive and apply the fundamental thermodynamic relations to thermal systems;
3. describe and explain the first and second laws of thermodynamics, and the concept of entropy;
4. state the assumptions of the kinetic theory and apply techniques of describing macroscopic
behaviour;
5. deduce the formalism of thermodynamics and apply it to simple systems in thermal
equilibrium; and
6. describe and determine the effect of forces and deformation of materials and surfaces.
Course Contents
Heat and temperature, temperature scales; gas laws; general gas equation; thermal conductivity;
first Law of thermodynamics; heat, work and internal energy, reversibility; thermodynamic
processes; adiabatic, isothermal, isobaric; second law of thermodynamics; heat engines and
entropy, Zero’s law of thermodynamics; kinetic theory of gases; molecular collisions and mean
free path; elasticity; Hooke's law, Young's shear and bulk moduli; hydrostatics; pressure,
buoyancy, Archimedes' principles; Bernoullis equation and incompressible fluid flow; surface
tension; adhesion, cohesion, viscosity, capillarity, drops and bubbles.
PHY 108: General Practical Physics II (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the student should be able to:
1. conduct measurements of some physical quantities;
2. make observations of events, collect and tabulate data;
3. identify and evaluate some common experimental errors;

262

4. plot and analyse graphs;
5. draw conclusions from numerical and graphical analysis of data; and
6. prepare and present practical reports.
Course Contents
This practical course is a continuation of PHY 107 and is intended to be taught during the second
semester of the 100 level to cover the practical aspect of the theoretical courses that have been
covered with emphasis on quantitative measurements, the treatment of measurement errors, and
graphical analysis. However, emphasis should be placed on the basic physical techniques for
observation, measurements, data collection, analysis and deduction.

ABUAD-CHM 102: General Chemistry II (2 Units C: LH=30)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is crucial to our university's mission of advancing Quality Education (SDG 4) and
fostering Innovation (SDG 9). It provides a comprehensive understanding of organic chemistry,
which is fundamental to multiple scientific disciplines, including pharmaceuticals, materials
science, and environmental science. The course highlights the role of organic chemistry in
developing new materials like fullerenes, which are essential for nanotechnology. By mastering
the concepts and techniques in organic chemistry, students are prepared to contribute to
innovative solutions in health, industry, and sustainability, addressing global challenges through
scientific advancement.
Overview
This course offers an in-depth study of organic chemistry, beginning with its historical
development and significance. It covers the electronic theory, structure determination,
nomenclature, and functional groups of organic compounds. Students will explore reaction
mechanisms, kinetics, stereochemistry, and the chemistry of hydrocarbons, alcohols, ethers,
amines, and other key organic compounds. Additionally, the course introduces the chemistry of
selected metals and non-metals, including comparative chemistry of group IA, IIA, and IVA
elements, and transition metal chemistry, with a focus on their practical applications in various
industries.
Objectives
1. understand the historical development and significance of organic chemistry.
2. describe the structure, nomenclature, and functional groups of organic compounds.

263

3. analyze reaction mechanisms, kinetics, and stereochemistry in organic reactions.
4. explore the chemistry of hydrocarbons, alcohols, ethers, amines, and other key organic
compounds.
5. examine the chemistry of selected metals, non-metals, and transition metals, including
their industrial applications.

Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the importance and development of organic chemistry;
2. define fullerenes and its applications;
3. discuss electronic theory;
4. determine the qualitative and quantitative of structures in organic chemistry;
5. state rules guiding nomenclature and functional group classes of organic chemistry;
6. determine the rate of reaction to predict mechanisms of reaction;
7. identify classes of organic functional group with brief description of their chemistry;
8. discuss comparative chemistry of group 1A, IIA and IVA elements; and
9. describe basic properties of transition metals.
Course Contents
Historical survey of the development and importance of organic chemistry; fullerenes as fourth
allotrope of carbon, uses as nanotubules, nanostructures, nanochemistry. Electronic theory in
organic chemistry. Isolation and purification of organic compounds; determination of structures
of organic compounds including qualitative and quantitative analysis in organic chemistry;
nomenclature and functional group classes of organic compounds. Introductory reaction
mechanism and kinetics. Stereochemistry. The chemistry of alkanes, alkenes, alkynes, alcohols,
ethers, amines, alkyl halides, nitriles, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids and derivatives. The
chemistry of selected metals and non-metals. Comparative chemistry of group IA, IIA and IVA
elements. Introduction to transition metal chemistry.

ABUAD- CHM 108: General Practical Chemistry II (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Senate-approved relevance

264

This practical course is integral to our university's focus on Quality Education (SDG 4) and
Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG 12). It builds on the foundation established in
CHM 107, enhancing students' proficiency in functional group analysis and quantitative analysis
using volumetric methods. These skills are essential for accurate chemical analysis in research
and industry, promoting sustainable practices in chemical production and environmental
monitoring. By mastering these techniques, students are better prepared to contribute to scientific
innovation and responsible resource management, aligning with global sustainability goals.
Overview
This course continues from CHM 107, offering advanced laboratory experiments in functional
group analysis and quantitative analysis through volumetric methods. Students will refine their
skills in identifying functional groups and conducting precise quantitative measurements. The
course emphasizes accurate data collection, analysis, and interpretation, which are critical for
success in research and industrial applications. By the end of the course, students will have a
deeper understanding of practical chemical analysis and its importance in various scientific
fields.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
1. perform advanced functional group analysis accurately.
2. conduct quantitative analysis using volumetric methods with precision.
3. analyze and interpret experimental data effectively.
4. develop proficiency in laboratory techniques for chemical analysis.
5. apply practical skills to real-world chemical problems and research.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the general laboratory rules and safety procedures;
2. collect scientific data and correctly carry out chemical experiments;
3. identify the basic glassware and equipment in the laboratory;
4. identify and carry out preliminary tests which include ignition, boiling point, melting point,
test on known and unknown organic compounds;
5. carry out solubility tests on known and unknown organic compounds;
6. carry out elemental tests on known and unknown compounds; and
7. carry out functional group/confirmatory test on known and unknown compounds which could
be acidic/basic/ neutral organic compounds.

265

Course Contents
Continuation of CHM 107. Additional laboratory experiments to include functional group
analysis, quantitative analysis using volumetric methods.

200 LEVEL FIRST SEMESTER
ENT 211: Entrepreneurship and Innovation (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, students should be able to:
1. explain the concepts and theories of entrepreneurship, intrapreneurship, opportunity seeking,
new value creation and risk-taking;
2. state the characteristics of an entrepreneur;
3. analyse the importance of micro and small businesses in wealth creation, employment
generation and financial independence;
4. engage in entrepreneurial thinking;
5. identify key elements in innovation;

Newton's laws of motion. Kinetic energy and momentum analyse

GET 209: Engineering Mathematics I (3 Units C: LH=45)

different engineering processes.
Course Contents
Limits, continuity, differentiation, introduction to linear first order differential equations, partial
and total derivatives, composite functions, matrices and determinants, vector algebra, vector
calculus, directional derivatives.

GET 204: Students Workshop Practice (2 Units C: LH=15; PH=45)

At the end of this course, the students should be able to:

6. determine household and industrial energy consumption, and understand practical energy
conservation measures.

Course Contents

experience in safe usage of tools and machines for selected tasks; Use of measuring instruments
(calipers, micrometers, gauges, sine bar, wood planners, saws, sanders, and pattern making).
Machine shop: lathe work shaping, milling, grinding, reaming, metal spinning. Hand tools, gas
and arc welding, cutting, brazing and soldering. Foundry practice. Industrial safety and accident
prevention, ergonomics, metrology. Casting processes. Metal forming processes: hot-working
and cold-working processes (forging, press-tool work, spinning, etc.). Metal joining processes
(welding, brazing and soldering). Heat treatment. Material removal processes. machine tools and
classification. Simple theory of metal cutting. Tool action and cutting forces. Introduction to CNC
machines. Supervised identification, use and care of various electrical and electronic components
such as resistors, inductors, capacitors, diodes and transistors. Exposure to different electric
circuits, wiring schemes, analogue and digital electrical and electronic measurements. Household
and industrial energy consumption measurements. Practical energy conservation principles.

271

GET 206: Fundamentals of Engineering Thermodynamics (3 Units C: LH=45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. describe basic concepts of thermodynamics, quantitative relations of Zeroth, first, second and
third laws;
2. define and explain system (surrounding, closed and open system), control volume and control
mass, extensive and intensive properties;
3. calculate absolute and gage pressure, and absolute temperature, calculate changes in kinetic,
potential, enthalpy and internal energy;
4. evaluate the properties of pure substances i.e. evaluate the state of the pure substances such as
compressed liquid, saturated liquid-vapour mixture and superheated vapour using property
diagrams and tables; arrange the ideal and real gas equations of state,
5. formulate the first law of thermodynamics for a closed system i.e. organize the change in energy
in the closed systems via heat and work transfer;
6. distinguish heat transfer by conduction, convection and radiation, and calculate the amount of
heat energy transferred;
7. calculate the changes in moving boundary work, spring work, electrical work and shaft work
in closed systems;
8. apply the first law of thermodynamics for closed systems and construct conservation of mass
and energy equations;
9. formulate the first law of thermodynamics to the open systems i.e. describe steady-flow open
system, apply the first law of thermodynamics to the nozzles, diffusers, turbines, compressors,
throttling valves, mixing chambers, heat exchangers, pipe and duct flow;
10. construct energy and mass balance for unsteady-flow processes;
11. evaluate thermodynamic applications using second law of thermodynamics;
12. calculate thermal efficiency and coefficient of performance for heat engine, refrigerators and
heat pumps; and
13. restate perpetual-motion machines, reversible and irreversible processes.
Course Contents
Basic concepts, definitions and laws (quantitative relations of Zeroth, first, second and third laws
of thermodynamics). Properties of pure substances: the two-property rule (P-V-T behaviour of
pure substances and perfect gases); state diagrams. The principle of corresponding state;
compressibility relations; reduced pressure; reduced volume; temperature; pseudo-critical
constants. The ideal gas: specific heat, polytropic processes. Ideal gas cycles; Carnot;
thermodynamic cycles, turbines, steam and gas, refrigeration. The first law of thermodynamics –

272

heat and work, applications to open and closed systems. The steady f low energy equation
(Bernoulli’s equation) and application. Second law of thermodynamics, heat cycles and
efficiencies.
GET 210: Engineering Mathematics II (3 Units C: LH=45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course, the students should be able to:
1. describe physical systems using ordinary differential equations (ODEs);
2. explain the practical importance of solving ODEs, solution methods, and analytically solve a
wide range of ODEs, including linear constant coefficient types;
3. numerically solve differential equations using MATLAB and other emerging applications; 4.
perform calculus operations on vector-valued functions, including derivatives, integrals,
curvature, displacement, velocity, acceleration, and torsion, as well as on functions of several
variables, including directional derivatives and multiple integrals;
5. solve problems using the fundamental theorem of line integrals, Green's theorem, the
divergence theorem, and Stokes' theorem, and perform operations with complex numbers; 6.
apply the concept and consequences of analyticity and the Cauchy-Riemann equations and of
results on harmonic and entire functions of complex variables, as well as the theory of conformal
mapping to solve problems from various fields of engineering; and
7. evaluate complex contour integrals directly and by the fundamental theorem, apply the Cauchy
integral theorem in its various versions, and the Cauchy integral formula.
Course Contents
Introduction to ordinary differential equations (ODEs); theory, applications, methods of solution;
second order differential equations. Advanced topics in calculus (vectors and vector valued
function, line integral, multiple integral and their applications). Elementary complex analysis
including functions of complex variables, limits and continuity. Derivatives, differentiation rules
and differentiation of integrals. Cauchy-Riemann equation, harmonic functions, basic theory of
conformal mapping, transformation and mapping and its applications to engineering problems.
Special functions.
