ABUAD-GET 208: Strength of Materials (3 Units C: LH=45)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is integral to our university's commitment to Quality Education (SDG 4) and Industry,
Innovation, and Infrastructure (SDG 9). It provides a deep understanding of the mechanical
behavior of materials under various loading conditions, which is essential for designing safe and
efficient structures. Mastery of these concepts is critical for students pursuing careers in civil,
mechanical, and aerospace engineering. The course equips students with the skills needed to
analyze and design structural components, contributing to innovative and sustainable
infrastructure development, essential for addressing global engineering challenges.
Overview
This course explores the principles of equilibrium, stress-strain relationships, and the application
of Hooke's law to various materials. It covers the effects of loading and temperature changes on
stresses and strains, torsion in circular members, and shear forces in beams. The course also
examines bending moments, bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined

274

loadings, and stress analysis. Students will gain the knowledge necessary to analyze and design
structural components under different conditions.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
1. understand the principles of equilibrium in structural systems.
2. analyze stress-strain relationships in materials using generalized Hooke's law.
3. evaluate stresses and strains due to different loading conditions and temperature changes.
4. understand and analyze torsion in circular members.
5. calculate shear forces and bending moments in beams under various loadings.
6. analyze bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined loadings.
7. apply stress analysis techniques to structural components.
8. design safe and efficient structural components considering various mechanical factors.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. recognize a structural system that is stable and in equilibrium;
2. determine the stress-strain relation for single and composite members based on Hooke's law;
3. estimate the stresses and strains in single and composite members due to temperature changes;
4. evaluate the distribution of shear forces and bending moments in beams with distributed and
concentrated loads;
5. determine bending stresses and their use in identifying slopes and deflections in beams;
6. use Mohr's circle to evaluate the normal and shear stresses in a multi-dimensional stress system
and transformation of these stresses into strains;
7. evaluate the stresses and strains due to torsion on circular members; and
8. determine the buckling loads of columns under various fixity conditions at the ends.
Course Contents
Consideration of equilibrium; composite members, stress-strain relation. Generalized Hooke's
law. Stresses and strains due to loading and temperature changes. Torsion of circular members.
Shear force, bending moments and bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined
loadings. Stress
Minimum Academic Standards
As stated in the CCMAS.

circuits, wiring and installation, etc. (8-10 weeks during the long vacation following 200 level).

and gas valuation. Mines and quarries valuation. Cost and schedule management- an engineering

400 LEVEL SECOND SEMESTER
ENG 490: Students Industrial Work Experience Scheme (6 Units)
A comprehensive internship programme in which students spend a full semester in approved
electrical engineering establishments (private and public) and industries. The exposure also
provide opportunity for students to sharpen their technical writing skill through field reports,
keeping log-books and prepared technical documents under close supervision of competent
lecturers. This will be registered alongside ENG 290 (SWEP I) and ENG 390 (SWEP II).

Practices and skills in general engineering through instruction in operation of hand and powered
tools for wood and metal cutting and fabrication. Supervised hand on experience in safe usage of
tools and machine for selected tasks. General practices on automobile repairs, survey, civil,
electrical and other related engineering practice. It is regarded as on the job experience in industry
chosen for practical working experience but not necessarily limited to the student’s major (12
weeks during the long vacation following 200 level).
ENG 390: Students Work Experience Programme (SWEP) II (3 UNITS)
On the job experience in industry chosen for practical working experience but not necessarily
limited to the student’s major (12 weeks during the long vacation following 300 level).
Eng 490 Students Industrial Work Experience Scheme (Siwes) (6 Units)
On the job experience in industry chosen for practical working experience but not necessarily
limited to the student’s major (24 weeks from the end of the first semester at 400-level to the
beginning of the first semester of the following session. Thus, the second semester at 400-level is
spent in industry.)

5.2. How the requirements of Complex Problem Solving (CPS) and Complex Engineering
Activities (CEA) have been addressed for Electrical Engineering programme
The Electrical Engineering programme addresses the requirements for Complex Problem Solving
(CPS) and Complex Engineering Activities (CEA) through an Outcome-Based Education (OBE)
framework that integrates these challenges across the curriculum, industrial training, hands-on
training and final year projects.
4.2.1. Integration into Programme Outcomes (POs)
The programme's 12 core outcomes are directly aligned with COREN attributes, explicitly
defining CPS and CEA as graduation requirements:
For CPS (PO1–PO4): Students must apply fundamental principles to identify, formulate, and
analyze complex engineering problems to reach substantiated conclusions and design systems
that meet specified needs.
For CEA (PO5, PO10): Students are required to select and apply modern engineering and IT tools
for complex activities and communicate effectively about these activities to both technical and
general audiences.
2. Coursework and Laboratory Scaffolding
The curriculum progressively increases problem complexity from 200-level to 500-level:

292

a) Foundational Level: Courses like Applied Mechanics (GET 207) and Basic Electrical

Engineering I (EEE 221) introduce students to identifying and solving engineering
problems using mathematical modeling and first principles.
b) Advanced Level: 500-level courses such as Power System Engineering (EEE 553) and

Control Engineering (EEE 561) require students to solve "complex operational challenges"
and analyze dynamic system stability under practical constraints.
c)

Modern Tool Usage: The Seminars on Engineering Software (EEE 564) specifically trains
students in using industry-standard tools (e.g., MATLAB, Altium, PSCAD) to simulate
and optimize complex electronic and power systems, satisfying the "Modern Tool Usage"
requirement for CEA.

4.2.2. Industrial Exposure (SIWES)
The Students Industrial Work Experience Scheme (SIWES III) is a critical vehicle for addressing
CPS and CEA:
a) Students spend 24 weeks in industry where they are motivated to identify industrial

practice engineering challenges and creatively devise impactful solutions.
b) The Engineering Project I (GET 402) course, taken during 400-level, specifically requires

students to complete the design phase of a complex engineering problem sourced directly
from their industry or community placement.
4.2.3. Final Year Project (EEE 599)
The Final Year Project is the primary assessment point for both CPS and CEA. Students must:
a) Develop a technology or system to solve a known and significant engineering problem.
b) Build or produce hardware/software solutions using the comprehensive skills acquired

throughout the five-year programme.
c)

Defend their work before a panel that includes an external assessor, demonstrating their
ability to communicate complex engineering activities (CEA) effectively

293

6.2.

Compliance with Admission Requirements
6.2.1

Inter-University Transfer:

a) Regulations:
Candidates from other accredited institutions can enroll in the Programme. The university
rule stipulates that every candidate must complete a minimum of two academic terms at
the institution to receive the university degree. Those possessing a Higher National
Diploma in a related area may qualify for entry into the 300 level. Each candidate is
assessed according to their own qualifications and achievements.
6.3.

Academic Regulations

The student handbook, published by the University and the Programme, is distributed to all
students. The University handbook includes entry requirements, rules and regulations that
govern examination conduct, the grading system, penalties for examination malpractice,
probation, expulsion, withdrawals, graduation, and other essential information students need to
understand regarding their academic program pursuit. The Programme handbook outlines
Programme Educational Objectives, Programme Outcomes, the Curriculum with its respective
Course Learning Outcomes and course contents for courses across all levels.

6.4.

Standard of Tests and Examinations

The programme adheres to the minimum standards established by the NUC when designing tests
and examinations. Throughout the semester, assignments and assessments are conducted to
cover the course materials as much as possible. At the conclusion of each semester, individual
instructors or a team of lecturers responsible for a course develop examination questions for that
subject, integrating Bloom’s Taxonomy into the questions. These questions must thoroughly
address the course content. Sample examination questions along with marking schemes for all
levels of the Programme have been provided. Examination questions from 100 level to 400 level
are jointly reviewed by selected co-examiners, consisting of senior academic staff within the
Programme. The final review is conducted by the Head of Programme, who is the Chief
Examiner. For the 500 level, examination questions undergo external moderation by Senateapproved External Examiner(s). At the end of the session, the External Examiner visits the
Programme to moderate the assessments of graduating students, including their final year
projects. Examinations take place at the end of each semester.
The final grade for each student consists of the examination score with continuous assessment
marks. The continuous assessment includes tasks such as assignments, term papers, mini
projects, and laboratory works. These assessments contribute 40% to the total grade, while the

296

examination score accounts for 60%. The duration of each course examination varies based on the
credit value assigned to the course. For a 3-credit course, seven questions are provided, and
students must answer five within a three-hour period. Similarly, for a 2-credit course, students
are required to answer four out of six questions in two hours. In their final year, students
undertake projects worth six units. This project extends throughout the entire session as a
continuous effort, with final project defense occurring at the end of the second semester. During
this time, students defend of their project through an oral examination. In the final semester,
presenting seminars with PowerPoint and demonstrating their projects are mandatory
requirements. The seminar plays a key role in enhancing the intellectual growth and confidence
of the students.
(a) Admission to the Examination
Every student who is duly registered for courses in a given semester is qualified to take the
examinations in those courses, except in the following cases (subject to Senate approval based on
College Board recommendation):
(i) a student who stays away from the University for more than three weeks without
official approval.
(ii) a student whose attendance at lectures or practical sessions in any course falls below
80 percent.
Before being allowed into the examination hall, each student must:
(i) present a valid University Identity Card along with an Examination Card.
(ii) ensure that no unauthorized or incriminating item is in their possession or among the
materials they legitimately brought into the hall, such as rulers, mathematical tables, and
similar items.
(iii) maintain orderly conduct and strictly follow all instructions given by the Invigilator
or Examiner.
(iv) refrain from any form of malpractice or attempt to engage in examination misconduct.
(b) Examination Marks
The marks awarded in each examination are determined by the internal examiners (Lecturers)
and subsequently endorsed by the examination moderators and the Chief Examiner (the HOD).
For final year students, the involvement of external examiners is also required during the
moderation process. The final stage of moderation takes place at the meeting of the Programme
Board of Examiners. Where there is a disagreement, the recommendation of the external

297

examiners may only be set aside if overruled by a two-thirds majority vote of the Examiners
Board.
(c)

Board of Examiners

The Programme Board of Examiners is made up of all academic staff within the Programme, with
the Head of Programme serving as the Chief Examiner. The College Board of Examiners, on the
other hand, comprises all members of the College Board of Studies together with any other
individuals assigned the responsibility of examining courses offered within the College.
(d)

Semester Load

Each student is required to register in line with the Programme guidelines, carrying not less than
15 credit units and not more than 24 credit units per semester. However, students who have are
in special cases may, with the approval of the Vice Chancellor, be allowed to register for more
than the maximum credit requirement.
(e)

Pass Mark

The pass mark in any course of is 45%.
(f)

Grading System

The performance of a student in each course shall be presented in both numerical scores and
corresponding letter grades. The overall academic standing of a student for a semester is
expressed as the Grade Point Average (GPA). The GPA is calculated by multiplying the Grade
Point (GP) earned in each course by the respective course credit unit (C) to obtain the Weighted
Grade Point (WGP). The total WGP for all courses taken in the semester is then divided by the
total number of registered credit units to determine the semester GPA.

The Cumulative Grade Point Average (CGPA) of a student is the overall average of the Weighted
Grade Points (WGP) accumulated across all semesters.
The procedure for computing the CGPA is as follows:
(a) Multiply the Grade Point (GP) obtained in each course by the corresponding course credit unit
C to determine the Weighted Grade Point (WGP).
(b) Add together all the WGP values for every course taken throughout the period of study.
(c) Divide the total WGP by the total credit units of all registered courses, whether passed or
failed, across all semesters.

Summary of the graduation requirements for the program

This section contains a summary of the process for ensuring and documenting that each graduate
completes all graduation requirements for the program
The curriculum for these degree programs is aligned with the approved COREN Benchmark
Minimum Academic Standard (BMAS). Comprehensive details are available in the program
handbook. To earn a Bachelor of Engineering (B.Eng.) degree in Electrical/Electronic Engineering
at Afe Babalola University, students must perform satisfactorily in Core/Compulsory courses,
Elective/Option courses, University requirements, Industrial Training, and adherence to National
Universities Commission (NUC) and COREN standards.
6.7.

question papers and marking guides. The University regards the external examination system as
an essential mechanism for maintaining quality assurance and promoting the highest standards
of professionalism. The present External Examiners are:

6.8.

Describe CQI strategies to be implemented in relation to students

Continuous Quality Improvement (CQI) strategies are designed to enhance the educational
experience and outcomes for students by continually assessing and refining various aspects of
300

the educational process. Several key areas are targeted when implementing CQI strategies related
to students:
Student Feedback: We actively gather feedback through surveys, focus groups, and
individual interviews to ensure our educational programs continuously evolve to meet
student needs. This invaluable input helps us identify areas for improvement, celebrate
program strengths, and create the best possible learning experience.
Learning Assessment: By employing a variety of assessments—including formative and
summative tasks, standardized tests, project-based work, and performance evaluations—
we gain a comprehensive understanding of student learning. Analyzing these results
enables us to identify areas needing improvement and design targeted interventions to
ensure all students succeed.
Curriculum Review: Our curriculum undergoes regular reviews to ensure alignment
with educational goals, industry demands, and evolving trends. This process includes
evaluating the curriculum's relevance, coherence, and effectiveness. We involve students
by gathering their feedback and incorporating their suggestions for improvement,
ensuring the program remains current and provides the best possible learning experience.
Student Engagement: The university fosters a vibrant learning environment by offering
a mix of experiential learning opportunities, collaborative projects, and extracurricular
activities. We actively track student participation to assess the impact of these initiatives
on student satisfaction, motivation, and academic success.
Through CQI strategies such as student feedback mechanisms, diverse assessments, and
curriculum reviews, educational institutions can cultivate a learning environment that
prioritizes student success and engagement. This ongoing process, fueled by student
input and data analysis, ensures programs remain relevant, effective, and ultimately
drives student satisfaction.
6.9.

Engineering Drawing Practice

Engineering drawing is one of the important courses taken by students and is a centrally
administered course by the College of Engineering. The college have drawing studios for the
drawing course in Engineering taken by all 200 level students.

6.10.

Adequacy of Lectures

There is a provisional lecture timetable and venues for all courses, which every lecturer follows
for their lectures. The university have a quality assurance unit that monitors the adequacy of
lectures.

301

6.11.

Record of Final Year Students Projects for Previous Sessions

The records of the final year student projects for the programme are available for perusal.

6.12. Mechanism for providing guidance to students of Electrical Engineering on academic,
career and aspects pertaining to wellness.
The Electrical/Electronics Engineering programme utilizes a multifaceted approach to student
guidance, ensuring that academic progress, professional growth, and personal well-being are
supported through structured institutional mechanisms, which include:
6.12.1 Academic Guidance and Mentorship
Academic support is primarily facilitated through the Academic Level Advisers system, where
specific faculty members are assigned to each cohort to provide personalized guidance on course
registration, academic performance, and adherence to university regulations. For the current
session, the advisers for the Electrical programme include Mr. V. Adebajo-Akinpelu (100L), Engr.
Dr. N. O. Omogbai (200L), Engr. T. E. Fabunmi (300L), Engr. Dr. T. O. Ejidokun (400L), and Dr. M.
A. Ogunlade (500L). Beyond administrative assistance, these advisers and the broader faculty act as
mentors, helping students align their learning trajectories with the Programme Educational
Objectives (PEOs) and providing instructional support to ensure the mastery of course learning
outcomes. Regular departmental and board meetings serve as platforms where student progress is
reviewed, and interventions are designed to address academic challenges democratically.
1. Career Development and Professional Placement
Career guidance is integrated into both the formal curriculum and the university’s administrative
services, offering students a roadmap for transitioning into the engineering industry. All students
have access to centralized career planning and placement services, which are supplemented by
departmental initiatives like "Career Week" and seminars featuring industry professionals.
Foundational courses such as Engineer in Society (GET 101) and Introduction to Electrical and
Electronics Engineering (EEE 102) introduce students to the diverse specializations and job profiles
within the field, ranging from Power and Manufacturing to Research and Software Engineering.
Furthermore, the Students Industrial Work Experience Scheme (SIWES) provides critical hands-on
exposure across three different levels (SIWES I, II, and III), allowing students to identify industrial
challenges and build professional networks before graduation. Professional identity is further
fostered through the IEEE and NSE staff advisories, currently coordinated by Engr. Dr. T. O.
Ejidokun, which encourage students to engage with global engineering communities.
2. Student Wellness and Counselling Services

302

The department recognizes that holistic student success depends on mental and emotional stability,
which is addressed through both departmental and college-level units. A dedicated Student
Counselling Unit at the College of Engineering, headed by Mr. Olapade, serves as the specialized
facility for students requiring psychological or emotional support. Within the department, student
well-being is monitored by the Welfare Officer, Engr. A. Akinola, who collaborates with level
advisers to identify and support students facing personal, health, or social difficulties. These support
mechanisms are designed to foster an inclusive and supportive environment that encourages
student engagement and success while ensuring that wellness issues do not become barriers to
academic achievement. Ethical conduct and personal integrity are also religiously practiced and
taught as part of the university’s guiding principles, further contributing to the character
development and overall wellness of the student body
6.12.2. Students’ Workload, Class Sizes for Theory, Laboratory Sessions and Completion of
Courses
For the Electrical Engineering programme, the management of student workload, class sizes, and
course completion follows the modularised Course Unit System and university regulations as
detailed hereafter.
1. Students’ Workload
The academic workload is determined by credit units assigned to each course within a 15-week
semester.
a) Credit Unit Definition: One credit unit is equivalent to one hour of lecture/tutorial per

week or three hours of laboratory, workshop, or studio work per week.
b) Semester Registration Limits: Students are required to register for a minimum of 15 credit

units and a maximum of 24 credit units per semester.
c)

Total Graduation Requirement: To be eligible for the award of the B.Eng. degree, UTME
students must complete between 130–138 core units and a total of approximately 197 units
over the five-year programme.

2. Class Sizes and Instructional Ratios
Class sizes are monitored to ensure effective teaching and access to laboratory facilities. The
following table summarizes the enrollment and instructional capacity for the Electrical programme:

3. Completion of Courses
Course completion is strictly governed by performance, attendance, and residency regulations.
a) Evaluation Breakdown: Completion of a course is based on a 40% Continuous Assessment

(quizzes, assignments, and mid-semester tests) and a 60% Final Examination.
b) Eligibility for Exams: Students must attain a minimum of 75% attendance to be eligible to

sit for the final examination in any course.
c)

Pass Criteria: The minimum pass mark is an aggregate of 45% in each course. To graduate,
students must pass all compulsory and departmental courses with a Cumulative Grade
Point Average (CGPA) of not less than 1.00.

d) Residency Limits: The maximum time allowed for completion of the degree is 15 academic

semesters for UTME entrants and 13 semesters for Direct Entry students
6.13. Students’ activities and involvement in student organizations that provide experience in
management and governance, representation in education and related matters and social
activities.
The programme provides several structured avenues for students to develop leadership,
management, and governance skills while engaging in a vibrant social and technical community.
6.13.1. Leadership and Management Experience
a) IEEE Student Body: Students actively lead the departmental branch of the Institute of

Electrical and Electronics Engineers (IEEE), with professional mentorship provided by the
IEEE Staff Adviser, Engr. Dr. T. O. Ejidokun. This involvement offers hands-on experience
in managing professional engineering activities and networking with global experts.
b) Team Leadership in Projects: The curriculum emphasizes group assignments and

capstone design projects, requiring students to rotate through leadership roles to manage
project lifecycles, meet deadlines, and coordinate multi-disciplinary teams.
304

c)

Engineering Management Training: Compulsory courses like Engineering Management
and Law (ENG 583) and Engineer in Society (GET 101) equip students with formal
principles of policy formulation, organizational structure, and financial management.

6.13.2. Educational Representation and Governance
a) Quality Improvement Participation: Students play a direct role in institutional governance

by participating in Continuous Quality Improvement (CQI) processes. They provide
critical feedback through end-of-semester surveys, focus groups, and interviews, which
are used to evaluate lecturer performance and refine the curriculum.
b) Stakeholder Engagement: Students are recognized as key stakeholders and are engaged

through interactive sessions and placement activities to ensure the programme meets their
evolving professional needs.
6.13.3. Technical Activities and Competitions
a) Innovation and Prototyping: Through the Salt Valley Tech Hub (referenced as the

Innovation and Enterprise Centre) and the Engineering Innovation and Fabrication Hub,
students engage in hackathons and real-world problem-solving.
b) Robotics: Students participate in advanced robotics activities, utilizing the Prototyping

Laboratory to design and implement automated systems, building on knowledge from
courses like Robotics and Automation (COE 519).
c)

Global Recognition: The programme’s focus on competitive technical excellence has led
students to win medals at prestigious international events such as the Microsoft
ImagineCup.

6.13.4. Social and Recreational Involvement
a) Campus Life and Wellness: The university fosters a holistic environment by integrating

social activities like movie nights at the planetarium and recreational events into the
student experience.
b) Support Facilities: Modern support facilities, including sports and recreational centers,

are provided to facilitate character building and enhance the overall quality of campus
life.

305

SECTION SEVEN:
CONTINUOUS QUALITY IMPROVEMENT

306

7.1.

The CQI Strategies

The process for planning and improving our Electrical/Electronic Engineering programme
includes how the curriculum was developed, review of its content, and response to feedback from
employers, industry experts, students, and alumni. We also track how individual courses
contribute to learning objectives and use assessments to measure programme outcomes. We
regularly review these objectives and continuously strive to improve the program's quality by:
•

Quality Teaching: The programme has a Quality Assurance (QA) committee dedicated
to ensuring all lecturers and technologists deliver high-quality instruction. They evaluate
lecturers and technologists' performance each semester and recommend improvements
for the next semester.

•

Research and Learning: Another committee encourages both faculty and students to
actively participate in research and share their findings. This committee helps find
research partners and supports publishing results in respected conferences, journals, and
seminars. Students and faculty also participate in seminars, conferences, workshops, and
industry visits to stay current and learn new things.

7.2.

Employer’s Rating of Graduates of the Programme/Department

Our programme has put in place the following to ensure our graduate has a good employers’
rating after graduation. Our department has made several recent improvements:
•

Research Focus: We launched a Research/Seminar/Conference and Workshop Committee
to boost collaboration and research output among staff and students. The mandate of this
committee includes equipping our final year students with requisite research
enhancement tools.

•

Quality Assurance: We established an Internal Quality Assurance Committee to ensure
yearly improvements in faculty development, teaching quality, and student learning.

•

Outcome-Based Education: We formed an OBE Implementation Committee in 2023 to
ensure we meet Outcome-Based Education requirements. The committee lead includes
the Head of Programme and a departmental OBE desk officer. Members of the Quality
Assurance Committee automatically join the OBE committee. This committee also
ensured that EEE 564, a course dedicated to the use of modern software for solving
electrical electronic engineering problems, is delivered to satisfaction.

•

Project Vetting: At the beginning of every academic session, Final Year Project Committee
reviewsthe students’ research interests and matches their proposed final-year project titles
307

to staff’s expertise to enhance the quality of project and reports. This initiative stemmed
from the OBE requirements.

Response to External Examiner’s Comments

Feedback from external experts is taken seriously by the university management. The
programme leadership carefully reviews comments from external examiners and discusses the
issues raised. We then decide on ways to address any weaknesses identified and ensure the
programme's strengths remain strong. Additionally, staff suggestions for improvement are
passed on to university management for further action.

313

COURSE CURRICULUM

314

Categorization of Courses
Courses are categorized as follows:
i. Compulsory Courses: Students must register for and pass before graduation.
ii. Elective Courses: Courses students may offer and pass to obtain the minimum units
required for graduation. Such courses are normally selected from within or outside the
programme with the approval of the department.
iii. Required Course: A course specified by a programme which a student shall take and
pass.
iv. Pre-requisite Courses: Courses students must take and pass before taking another
specified course.
v. Institutional Courses: Courses whose knowledge is essential for the preparation of
Afe Babalola University for future employment after graduation.
vi. Carry-over Courses: On the account of any failure by a student to satisfy a pass
requirement in any course, such student will be required to repeat only the
course/courses the following academic session or re-write the prescribed examination in
such course at the end of the academic session.
Students carrying over courses shall not be allowed to register for more than the
approved maximum number of credits for the semester.

COURSE DESCRIPTION
100 LEVEL
GST 111: Communication in English (2 Units C: LH=15; PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, students should be able to:
1. identify possible sound patterns in English Language;
2. list notable language skills;
3. classify word formation processes;
320

4. construct simple and fairly complex sentences in English;
5. apply logical and critical reasoning skills for meaningful presentations;
6. demonstrate an appreciable level of the art of public speaking and listening; and
7. write simple and technical reports.
Course Contents
Sounds and sound patterns in English Language (vowels and consonants, phonetics and
phonology). English word classes (lexical and grammatical words, definitions, forms, functions,
usages, collocations). major word formation processes; the sentence in English (types: structural
and functional). grammar and usage (tense, concord and modality). Reading and types of
reading, comprehension skills, 3RsQ. Logical and critical thinking; reasoning methods (logic and
syllogism, inductive and deductive argument, analogy, generalisation and explanations). Ethical
considerations, copyright rules and infringements. Writing activities: pre-writing (brainstorming
and outlining). writing (paragraphing, punctuation and expression). post- writing (editing and
proofreading). Types of writing (summary, essays, letter, curriculum vitae, report writing, notemaking) etc. Mechanics of writing. Information and Communication Technology in modern
language learning. Language skills for effective communication. The art of public speaking.
GET 101: Engineer in Society (1 Unit C: LH=15)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. differentiate between science, engineering and technology, and relate them to innovation;
2. distinguish between the different cadres of engineering – engineers, technologists, technicians
and craftsmen and their respective roles and competencies;
3. identify and distinguish between the relevant professional bodies in engineering;
4. categorise the goals of global development or sustainable development goals (SDGs); and
5. identify and evaluate safety and risk in engineering practice.
Course Contents
History, evolution and philosophy of science. engineering and technology. The engineering
profession – engineering family (engineers, technologists, technicians and craftsmen),
professional bodies and societies. Engineers' code of conduct and ethics, and engineering literacy.
Sustainable development goals (SDGs), innovation, infrastructures and nation building economy, politics, business. Safety and risk analysis in engineering practice. Engineering
competency skills – curriculum overview, technical, soft and digital skills. Guest seminars and
invited lectures from different engineering professional associations.
MTH 101: Elementary Mathematics I (Algebra and Trigonometry)
(2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course students should be able to:
1. define and explain set, subset, union, intersection, complements, and demonstrate the use of
Venn diagrams;
321

2. solve quadratic equations;
3. solve trigonometric functions;
4. identify various types of numbers; and
5. solve some problems using binomial theorem.
Course Contents
Elementary set theory, subsets, union, intersection, complements, Venn diagrams. Real numbers,
integers, rational and irrational numbers. Mathematical induction, real sequences and series,
theory of quadratic equations, binomial theorem, complex numbers, algebra of complex numbers,
the argand diagram. De-Moiré’s theorem, nth roots of unity. Circular measure, trigonometric
functions of angles of any magnitude, addition and factor formulae.
PHY 101: General Physics I (Mechanics) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the students should be able to:
1. identify and deduce the physical quantities and their units;
2. differentiate between vectors and scalars;
3. describe and evaluate motion of systems on the basis of the fundamental laws of mechanics;
4. apply Newton’s laws to describe and solve simple problems of motion;
5. evaluate work, energy, velocity, momentum, acceleration, and torque of moving or rotating
objects;
6. explain and apply the principles of conservation of energy, linear and angular momentum;
7. describe the laws governing motion under gravity; and
8. explain motion under gravity and quantitatively determine behaviour of objects moving under
gravity.
Course Contents
Space and time; units and dimension, vectors and scalars, differentiation of vectors: displacement,
velocity and acceleration; kinematics; Newton’s laws of motion (inertial frames, impulse, force
and action at a distance, momentum conservation); relative motion; application of Newtonian
mechanics; equations of motion; conservation principles in physics, conservative forces,
conservation of linear momentum, kinetic energy and work, potential energy, system of particles,
centre of mass; rotational motion; torque, vector product, moment, rotation of coordinate axes
and angular momentum. Polar coordinates; conservation of angular momentum; circular motion;
moments of inertia, gyroscopes and precession; gravitation: Newton’s law of gravitation, Kepler’s
laws of planetary motion, gravitational potential energy, escape velocity, satellites motion and
orbits.
PHY 107: General Practical Physics I (1 Unit C: PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the student should be able to:
1. conduct measurements of some physical quantities;
2. make observations of events, collect and tabulate data;
322

3. identify and evaluate some common experimental errors;
4. plot and analyse graphs; and
5. draw conclusions from numerical and graphical analysis of data.
Course Contents
This introductory course emphasizes quantitative measurements. Experimental techniques. The
treatment of measurement errors. Graphical analysis. The experiments include studies of meters,
the oscilloscope, mechanical systems, electrical and mechanical resonant systems, light, heat,
viscosity, etc. (covered in PHY 101, 102, 103 and PHY 104). However, emphasis should be placed
on the basic physical techniques for observation, measurements, data collection, analysis, and
deduction.

EEE 102: Introduction to Electrical and Electronics Engineering (2 units C: LH=15)
Learning Outcomes
Students will be able to:
1. comprehend the duties and functions of an Electrical and Electronics Engineer (EEE);
2. state the requirements for the profession and career opportunities;
3. state the careers related to EEE; and
4. explain the future of EEE.
Course Contents
History of Electrical Engineering. Evolution of EEE. Duties of EE Engineers. Areas of
specialisation and work environment. Skill requirements (soft and hard). Qualities for EE
Engineers. Careers related to EEE. Typical course modules. Job outlook/opportunities for EE
Engineers. Future of EEE. Professional registration (NSE, COREN, IEEE, IET, etc.). Passive
components (R, L, C, transformers): descriptive features, including values and colour codes, uses
in electrical circuits.DC and AC signal parameters
ABUAD-CHM 101: General Chemistry I (2 Units C: LH=30)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is essential for students at our university as it builds a strong foundation in chemistry,
aligning with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 4 (Quality Education) and 9 (Industry,
Innovation, and Infrastructure). A deep understanding of chemical principles is crucial for
advancing in scientific research, environmental sustainability, and technological innovation. By
mastering the content, students will be equipped to contribute to sustainable industrial practices,
environmental conservation, and scientific advancements, directly impacting global challenges.
This course fosters critical thinking and problem-solving skills, preparing students to be leaders
in science, technology, and sustainability.
Overview
323

This course introduces the fundamental concepts of chemistry, including atoms, molecules,
elements, and compounds. It explores modern electronic theory, electronic configuration, and
periodicity, and examines molecular structures, chemical bonding, and stoichiometry. Students
will also study the kinetic theory of matter, thermochemistry, reaction rates, equilibrium, and
thermodynamics. Additionally, the course covers the properties of gases, acids, bases, redox
reactions, electrochemistry, and radioactivity, providing a comprehensive understanding of
essential chemical principles.
Objectives
6. understand and describe the basic structure of atoms, molecules, elements, and compounds.
7. apply electronic configuration and periodicity to explain chemical properties.
8. analyze and predict the shapes and structures of molecules and solids.
9. solve chemical equations and apply principles of stoichiometry, thermodynamics, and
kinetics.
10. understand and apply concepts of redox reactions, electrochemistry, and radioactivity.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. define atom, molecules and chemical reactions;
2. discuss the modern electronic theory of atoms;
3. write electronic configurations of elements on the periodic table;
4. rationalise the trends of atomic radii, ionisation energies, electronegativity of the elements,
based on their position in the periodic table;
5. identify and balance oxidation–reduction equation and solve redox titration problems;
6. draw shapes of simple molecules and hybridised orbitals;
7. identify the characteristics of acids, bases and salts, and solve problems based on their
quantitative relationship;
8. apply the principles of equilibrium to aqueous systems using LeChatelier’s principle to predict
the effect of concentration, pressure and temperature changes on equilibrium mixtures;
9. analyse and perform calculations with the thermodynamic functions, enthalpy, entropy and
free energy; and
10. determine rates of reactions and its dependence on concentration, time and temperature.
Course Contents
Atoms, molecules, elements and compounds, and chemical reactions. Modern electronic theory
of atoms. Electronic configuration, periodicity and building up of the periodic table.
Hybridisation and shapes of simple molecules. Valence forces; Structure of solids. Chemical
equations and stoichiometry; chemical bonding and intermolecular forces, kinetic theory of
matter. Elementary thermochemistry; rates of reaction, equilibrium and thermodynamics. Acids,
bases and salts. Properties of gases. Redox reactions and introduction to electrochemistry.
Radioactivity.
ABUAD-CHM 107: General Practical Chemistry I (1 Unit C: PH 45)
324

Senate-approved relevance
This laboratory course is integral to our university's commitment to Quality Education (SDG 4)
and Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG 12). It provides hands-on experience in
fundamental chemical techniques, reinforcing theoretical knowledge from CHM 101. By
mastering these practical skills, students develop the ability to conduct accurate experiments,
analyze data, and present findings effectively—essential competencies for careers in science,
industry, and research. The course also promotes safe and sustainable laboratory practices,
preparing students to contribute to sustainable industrial processes and environmental
stewardship.
Overview
This laboratory course offers practical experience in key chemical techniques, complementing the
theoretical content of CHM 101. Students will engage in acid-base titrations, qualitative analysis,
redox reactions, and gravimetric analysis. Emphasis is placed on accurate data collection,
analysis, and presentation, fostering essential laboratory skills. The course prepares students to
apply these techniques in scientific research and industry, ensuring a comprehensive
understanding of experimental chemistry.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
6. perform acid-base titrations with precision and accuracy.
7. conduct qualitative and quantitative analysis of chemical substances.
8. understand and execute redox reactions and gravimetric analysis.
9. analyze and interpret experimental data effectively.
10. present laboratory findings clearly and professionally.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the general laboratory rules and safety procedures;
2. collect scientific data and correct carry out chemical experiments;
3. identify the basic glassware and equipment in the laboratory;
4. state the differences between primary and secondary standards;
5. perform redox titration;
6. record observations and measurements in the laboratory notebooks; and
7. analyse the data to arrive at scientific conclusions.
Course Contents
Laboratory experiments designed to reflect topics presented in courses CHM 101 and CHM 102.
These include acid-base titrations, qualitative analysis, redox reactions, gravimetric analysis, data
analysis and presentation.
GST 112: Nigerian Peoples and Cultures (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the completion of the course, the students are expected to be able:
325

1. analyse the historical foundation of Nigerian cultures and arts in pre-colonial times;
2. identify and list the major linguistic groups in Nigeria;
3. explain the gradual evolution of Nigeria as a political entity;
4. analyse the concepts of trade and economic self-reliance of Nigerian peoples in relation to
national development;
5. enumerate the challenges of the Nigerian state regarding nation building;
6. analyse the role of the Judiciary in upholding fundamental human rights
7. identify the acceptable norms and values of the major ethnic groups in Nigeria; and
8. list possible solutions to identifiable Nigerian environmental, moral and value problems.
Course Contents
Nigerian history, culture and art up to 1800 (Yoruba, Hausa and Igbo peoples and cultures;
peoples and cultures of the minority ethnic groups). Nigeria under colonial rule (advent of
colonial rule in Nigeria; colonial administration of Nigeria). Evolution of Nigeria as a political
unit (amalgamation of Nigeria in 1914; formation of political parties in Nigeria; nationalist
movement and struggle for independence). Nigeria and challenges of nation building (military
intervention in Nigerian politics; Nigerian Civil War). Concepts of trade and economics of selfreliance (indigenous trade and market system; indigenous apprenticeship system among
Nigerian peoples; trade, skill acquisition and self-reliance). Social justice and national
development (definition and classification of law); Judiciary and fundamental rights. Individuals,
norms and values (basic Nigerian norms and values, patterns of citizenship acquisition;
citizenship and civic responsibilities; indigenous languages, usage and development; negative
attitudes and conducts [Cultism, kidnapping and other related social vices]). Re-orientation,
moral and national values (The 3Rs – Reconstruction, Rehabilitation and Re-orientation; reorientation strategies: Operation Feed the Nation (OFN), Green Revolution, Austerity Measures,
War Against Indiscipline and Corruption (WAIC), Mass Mobilization for Self-Reliance, Social
Justice and Economic Recovery (MAMSER), National Orientation Agency (NOA). Current sociopolitical and cultural developments in Nigeria.
GET 102: Engineering Graphics and Solid Modelling I (2 Units C: LH=15; PH 45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. have a good grasp of design thinking and be obsessed with the determination to apply such to
solving simple every day and also complex problems;
2. recognize the fundamental concepts of engineering drawing and graphics;
3. show skills to represent the world of engineering objects in actionable solid models, and put
such models in a form where they can be inputs for simulation and analyses;
4. analyse such models for strength and cost.
5. prepare the objects for modern production and manufacturing techniques of additive and
subtractive manufacturing;
6. recognise that engineering is multidisciplinary in the sense that mechanical, electrical and other
parts of physical structures are modelled in context as opposed to the analytical nature of the
courses they take;and
326

7. analyse and master the basics of mechanical and thermal loads in engineering systems.
Course Contents
Introduction to design thinking and engineering graphics. First and third angle orthogonal
projections. Isometric projections; sectioning, conventional practices, conic sections and
development. Freehand and guided sketching – pictorial and orthographic. Visualisation and
solid modelling in design, prototyping and product-making. User interfaces in concrete terms.
Design, drawing, animation, rendering and simulation work spaces. Sketching of 3D objects.
Viewports and sectioning to shop drawings in orthographic projections and perspectives.
Automated viewports. Sheet metal and surface modelling. Material selection and rendering. This
course will use latest professional design tools such as fusion 360, solid works, solid edge or
equivalent.
MTH 102: Elementary Mathematics II (Calculus) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of the course, students should be able to:
1. identify the types of rules in differentiation and integration;
2. recognise and understand the meaning of function of a real variable, graphs, limits and
continuity;
3. solve some applications of definite integrals in areas and volumes;
4. solve function of a real variable, plot relevant graphs, identify limits and idea of continuity;
5. identify the derivative as limit of rate of change;
6. identify techniques of differentiation and perform extreme curve sketching;
7. identify integration as an inverse of differentiation;
8. identify methods of integration and definite integrals; and
9. perform integration application to areas, volumes.
Course Contents
Functions of a real variable, graphs, limits and idea of continuity. The derivative, as limit of rate
of change. Techniques of differentiation, maxima and minima. Extreme curve sketching,
integration, definite integrals, reduction formulae, application to areas, volumes (including
approximate integration: Trapezium and Simpson's rule).
PHY 102: General Physics II (Behaviour of Matter) (2 Units C: LH=30)
Learning Outcomes
On completion, the students should be able to:
1. explain the concepts of heat and temperature and relate the temperature scales;
2. define, derive and apply the fundamental thermodynamic relations to thermal systems;
3. describe and explain the first and second laws of thermodynamics, and the concept of entropy;
4. state the assumptions of the kinetic theory and apply techniques of describing macroscopic
behaviour;
5. deduce the formalism of thermodynamics and apply it to simple systems in thermal
equilibrium; and
327

6. describe and determine the effect of forces and deformation of materials and surfaces.
Course Contents
Heat and temperature, temperature scales; gas laws; general gas equation; thermal conductivity;
first Law of thermodynamics; heat, work and internal energy, reversibility; thermodynamic
processes; adiabatic, isothermal, isobaric; second law of thermodynamics; heat engines and
entropy, Zero’s law of thermodynamics; kinetic theory of gases; molecular collisions and mean
free path; elasticity; Hooke's law, Young's shear and bulk moduli; hydrostatics; pressure,
buoyancy, Archimedes' principles; Bernoullis equation and incompressible fluid flow; surface
tension; adhesion, cohesion, viscosity, capillarity, drops and bubbles.

On completion, the student should be able to:
1. conduct measurements of some physical quantities;

covered with emphasis on quantitative measurements, the treatment of measurement errors, and

ABUAD-CHM 102: General Chemistry II (2 Units C: LH=30)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is crucial to our university's mission of advancing Quality Education (SDG 4) and
fostering Innovation (SDG 9). It provides a comprehensive understanding of organic chemistry,
which is fundamental to multiple scientific disciplines, including pharmaceuticals, materials
science, and environmental science. The course highlights the role of organic chemistry in
developing new materials like fullerenes, which are essential for nanotechnology. By mastering
the concepts and techniques in organic chemistry, students are prepared to contribute to
innovative solutions in health, industry, and sustainability, addressing global challenges through
scientific advancement.
Overview

328

This course offers an in-depth study of organic chemistry, beginning with its historical
development and significance. It covers the electronic theory, structure determination,
nomenclature, and functional groups of organic compounds. Students will explore reaction
mechanisms, kinetics, stereochemistry, and the chemistry of hydrocarbons, alcohols, ethers,
amines, and other key organic compounds. Additionally, the course introduces the chemistry of
selected metals and non-metals, including comparative chemistry of group IA, IIA, and IVA
elements, and transition metal chemistry, with a focus on their practical applications in various
industries.
Objectives
6. understand the historical development and significance of organic chemistry.
7. describe the structure, nomenclature, and functional groups of organic compounds.
8. analyze reaction mechanisms, kinetics, and stereochemistry in organic reactions.
9. explore the chemistry of hydrocarbons, alcohols, ethers, amines, and other key organic
compounds.
10. examine the chemistry of selected metals, non-metals, and transition metals, including their
industrial applications.

Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. state the importance and development of organic chemistry;
2. define fullerenes and its applications;
3. discuss electronic theory;
4. determine the qualitative and quantitative of structures in organic chemistry;
5. state rules guiding nomenclature and functional group classes of organic chemistry;
6. determine the rate of reaction to predict mechanisms of reaction;
7. identify classes of organic functional group with brief description of their chemistry;
8. discuss comparative chemistry of group 1A, IIA and IVA elements; and
9. describe basic properties of transition metals.
Course Contents
Historical survey of the development and importance of organic chemistry; fullerenes as fourth
allotrope of carbon, uses as nanotubules, nanostructures, nanochemistry. Electronic theory in
organic chemistry. Isolation and purification of organic compounds; determination of structures
of organic compounds including qualitative and quantitative analysis in organic chemistry;
nomenclature and functional group classes of organic compounds. Introductory reaction
mechanism and kinetics. Stereochemistry. The chemistry of alkanes, alkenes, alkynes, alcohols,
ethers, amines, alkyl halides, nitriles, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids and derivatives. The
chemistry of selected metals and non-metals. Comparative chemistry of group IA, IIA and IVA
elements. Introduction to transition metal chemistry.

ABUAD- CHM 108: General Practical Chemistry II (1 Unit C: PH 45)

deeper understanding of practical chemical analysis and its importance in various scientific
fields.
Objectives

6. perform advanced functional group analysis accurately.

GET 206: Fundamentals of Engineering Thermodynamics (3 Units C: LH=45)
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
336

1. describe basic concepts of thermodynamics, quantitative relations of Zeroth, first, second and
third laws;
2. define and explain system (surrounding, closed and open system), control volume and control
mass, extensive and intensive properties;
3. calculate absolute and gage pressure, and absolute temperature, calculate changes in kinetic,
potential, enthalpy and internal energy;
4. evaluate the properties of pure substances i.e. evaluate the state of the pure substances such as
compressed liquid, saturated liquid-vapour mixture and superheated vapour using property
diagrams and tables; arrange the ideal and real gas equations of state,
5. formulate the first law of thermodynamics for a closed system i.e. organize the change in energy
in the closed systems via heat and work transfer;
6. distinguish heat transfer by conduction, convection and radiation, and calculate the amount of
heat energy transferred;
7. calculate the changes in moving boundary work, spring work, electrical work and shaft work
in closed systems;
8. apply the first law of thermodynamics for closed systems and construct conservation of mass
and energy equations;
9. formulate the first law of thermodynamics to the open systems i.e. describe steady-flow open
system, apply the first law of thermodynamics to the nozzles, diffusers, turbines, compressors,
throttling valves, mixing chambers, heat exchangers, pipe and duct flow;
10. construct energy and mass balance for unsteady-flow processes;
11. evaluate thermodynamic applications using second law of thermodynamics;
12. calculate thermal efficiency and coefficient of performance for heat engine, refrigerators and
heat pumps; and
13. restate perpetual-motion machines, reversible and irreversible processes.
Course Contents
Basic concepts, definitions and laws (quantitative relations of Zeroth, first, second and third laws
of thermodynamics). Properties of pure substances: the two-property rule (P-V-T behaviour of
pure substances and perfect gases); state diagrams. The principle of corresponding state;
compressibility relations; reduced pressure; reduced volume; temperature; pseudo-critical
constants. The ideal gas: specific heat, polytropic processes. Ideal gas cycles; Carnot;
thermodynamic cycles, turbines, steam and gas, refrigeration. The first law of thermodynamics –
heat and work, applications to open and closed systems. The steady f low energy equation
(Bernoulli’s equation) and application. Second law of thermodynamics, heat cycles and
efficiencies.

ABUAD-GET 208: Strength of Materials (3 Units C: LH=45)
Senate-approved relevance
This course is integral to our university's commitment to Quality Education (SDG 4) and Industry,
Innovation, and Infrastructure (SDG 9). It provides a deep understanding of the mechanical
behavior of materials under various loading conditions, which is essential for designing safe and
efficient structures. Mastery of these concepts is critical for students pursuing careers in civil,
mechanical, and aerospace engineering. The course equips students with the skills needed to
analyze and design structural components, contributing to innovative and sustainable
infrastructure development, essential for addressing global engineering challenges.
Overview
This course explores the principles of equilibrium, stress-strain relationships, and the application
of Hooke's law to various materials. It covers the effects of loading and temperature changes on
stresses and strains, torsion in circular members, and shear forces in beams. The course also
examines bending moments, bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined
loadings, and stress analysis. Students will gain the knowledge necessary to analyze and design
structural components under different conditions.
Objectives
The objectives of the course are to:
1. understand the principles of equilibrium in structural systems.
2. analyze stress-strain relationships in materials using generalized Hooke's law.
3. evaluate stresses and strains due to different loading conditions and temperature changes.
339

4. understand and analyze torsion in circular members.
5. calculate shear forces and bending moments in beams under various loadings.
6. analyze bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined loadings.
7. apply stress analysis techniques to structural components.
8. design safe and efficient structural components considering various mechanical factors.
Learning Outcomes
At the end of this course, the students should be able to:
1. recognize a structural system that is stable and in equilibrium;
2. determine the stress-strain relation for single and composite members based on Hooke's law;
3. estimate the stresses and strains in single and composite members due to temperature changes;
4. evaluate the distribution of shear forces and bending moments in beams with distributed and
concentrated loads;
5. determine bending stresses and their use in identifying slopes and deflections in beams;
6. use Mohr's circle to evaluate the normal and shear stresses in a multi-dimensional stress system
and transformation of these stresses into strains;
7. evaluate the stresses and strains due to torsion on circular members; and
8. determine the buckling loads of columns under various fixity conditions at the ends.
Course Contents
Consideration of equilibrium; composite members, stress-strain relation. Generalized Hooke's
law. Stresses and strains due to loading and temperature changes. Torsion of circular members.
Shear force, bending moments and bending stresses in beams with symmetrical and combined
loadings. Stress
Minimum Academic Standards
As stated in the CCMAS.

EEE 576: Microcomputer Technology (2, 1, 0) 3 Units
Elements of digital computer design; control unit, micro-programming, bus organization and
addressing schemes. Micro-processors, system architecture, bus control, instruction execution
and addressing modes.
Machine codes, assembly language and high-level language
programming, Micro-processors as state machines. Microprocessor interfacing: Input/output.
Technique, interrupt systems and direct memory access; interfacing to analogue systems and
applications to D/A and A/D converters. System development tools: simulators, EPROM
programming, assemblers and loaders, overview of an available microprocessor application.
EEE 578: Broadcasting and Internet Technology (2, 1, 0) 3 Units
Elements of broadcasting system, studio equipment, microphones, sound, disc, magnetic tape
recording, and radio transmitters power amplifiers, FM systems, radiating antenna, television
camera, synchronization circuits, video amplifiers. Broadcasting regulations. Frequency
spectrum: allocation, assignment and licensing. Regulatory bodies. Design, configuration, and
services of CATV, MATV, MMDS systems. Multipath problems. Polarization, field strength, and
footprint. Transmitter power rating, beam-width, interference and minimum separation. Digital
Television standards: MPEG, DVB, channel coding techniques. HDTV. Digital television/monitor
set: LCD, and Plasma technology. Internet Technology: The Internet, definition and services.
Internet architecture, OSI layers, TCP/IP, Internet addressing, IPv4, IPv6. Internet broadcasting:
principles, components, standards, and applications.

354

SECTION EIGHT: STAFFING

355

8.1.

Recruitment, Retention, Dismissal and Welfare Recruitment

Appointment to any established staff position in the University is normally through
advertisements and interviews. The Registrar compiles the list of short-listed candidates from
various Departments for consideration and approval of the Vice-Chancellor.
Short-listed candidates are interviewed by the Assessment and Interview Panel. Successful
candidates are put before the Appointments and Promotions Committee (APC) for consideration
and approval on behalf of the Governing Council.
The APC is charged with the responsibility of all matters concerning promotion and appointment
of different cadres of Senior Staff (both teaching and non-teaching), matters relating to salaries
and wages, general conditions of service for all staff, confirmation, or extension of appointment,
and to advise on any other matters referred to it by Council or Senate. The membership of the
APC consists of the Vice-Chancellor, two Council representatives (who are not members of
Senate), the Registrar, the University Librarian, Bursar, Provosts of Faculty's, and Directors of
Academic Units.
Retention and Welfare
The University cares for and therefore retains its staff through cordial relationship with
Administration. Salaries and allowances are the same as for the Federal universities and are paid
regularly. Indeed, there has not been a situation when salaries are delayed. Also, funds are made
available for staff development, attendance at conferences, research grants, etc.
Dismissal
There is a Senior Staff Disciplinary Committee (SSDC), which is a joint Committee of Council and
Senate, whose duties are to investigate and report on any staff disciplinary matters referred to by
Council or by the Vice-Chancellor. However, any staff charged with official misconduct is given
a chance of a fair hearing by the SSDC before further action is taken against such staff.

8.7.

Overall Staff Workload

This section discusses how the overall staff workload enables effective teaching (including
student-teacher ratio), student-staff interaction, student advising and counselling, institutional
service and research activities, professional development and interaction with industry
8.7.1. Workload and effective teaching
The Electrical/Electronic Engineering program emphasizes maintaining a balanced workload for
faculty, as detailed in Tables 8.7. This balance allows lecturers to dedicate ample time to students.
As mentioned in section 8.1, the university employs highly qualified instructors. Consistent
positive feedback on teaching effectiveness is reflected in student evaluations and faculty
monitoring.
Lectures are meticulously designed to achieve program outcomes (POs) and course learning
outcomes (CLOs). Instructors employ a variety of teaching methods to enhance student
engagement, including course notes, electronic smart boards, whiteboards, and multimedia
facilities. This diverse approach caters to different learning styles and fosters a dynamic learning
environment.

3

Staff Development Programme

Afe Babalola University prides herself in her staff growth and development some of the means
by which this is supported by the university are detailed below.
8.10.1. Staff Development Award
All members of staff on a permanent appointment are eligible for consideration for the
staff development award as appropriate. The scheme is managed by the Appointments
and Promotions Committee. A member of staff who has benefited from the staff
development award is bonded to the University for two years for every year of award
subject to a maximum of five years.
8.10.2. Short-term Training Programme
The University encourages and sponsors short-term training programme including short
courses, seminars, workshops, colloquia etc. each lasting not more than four weeks. These
programme, which are for both teaching and non-teaching staff, are covered under the
Staff Development Scheme and managed by the Appointments and Promotions
Committee.
8.10.3. Learned Conferences
The University encourages eligible staff to participate in learned conferences and
normally funds the participation of at least one local learned conference per annum for
every academic staff, subject to availability of funds.

A few have enjoyed part-

sponsorship for overseas learned conferences.
8.10.4 Research Grants
The University has the policy to offer research grants to eligible academic staff to conduct
their research, except that the award is not for studies towards higher degree awards.

8.13.

Staff Contribution to Engineering and Industry

Some members of staff of the department are actively contributing to engineering and industry.
The contribution of our faculty to the advancement of engineering and to Industry which
included but not limited to proffering solution to real-life problems in the community and in
industry, making breakthrough in ground-breaking research leading to the presentation of
papers in Conferences and in Engineering associations. And some are very active in professional
associations such as NSE and COREN.
Table 8.13 lists some of the contributions of the Departmental Staffs to Engineering and the
Industry.

8.15.

Administration of the Programme

Electrical/Electronic Engineering is headed by the Head of Department (HOD). Level Advisors,
course coordinators, Examination officers, Undergraduate Project Coordinator, SIWES
coordinators, and Facility Manager, are appointed to work with the HOD for the smooth running
of the programme.
8.16.

Involvement of Staff in the Decision-making Process and General Administration

Decision-making process is through regular Departmental and Committee meetings. The Head
of Department is the chairman at departmental meetings and in such meetings, major academic
and non-academic decisions are democratically taken. At such meetings, ad hoc committees can
be constituted to carry out certain activities and submit their findings to the programme for final
deliberations and decisions.
The Head of Department at the Programme Board Meeting appoints the Level Advisers, Course
Coordinators, and Examination Officers from among his staff to head these sub-units, within the
programme. During Programme Board meetings, most issues deliberated upon are decided
based on consensus or democratic ethos in line with practices that the chair considers to be in
conformity with the university quality assurance and academic standards.
It is also the Head of Programme who assigns (allocates) courses to individual lecturers with
396

input from other lecturers at the Programme Board meetings, usually based on their areas of
specialization as informed by their CVs submitted to the programme. The course allocation
system is based on shared interest and or collective efforts, that is, more than one person can team
up to teach a course with one of them (most likely the senior person) as coordinator. The Head of
Programme also allocates students to staff for supervision of their Final year Projects.

8.18.

Faculty and Programme Board

The Faculty of Engineering Board consists of the Dean as chairman, all the Heads of Programme,
all Professors and Associate Professors and lecturers in the Faculty. Subject to the General
Regulations of the University, the Programme Board is responsible for all academic and nonacademic matters affecting the programme. The Board comprises of the Head of Programme (as
the Chairman), all Lecturers cadre.
397

8.19.

Staff Promotions

One of the University’s objectives is to attract, develop and retain high quality staff. Academic
promotion seeks to recognize and reward excellent staff. All academic staff are eligible and
encouraged to apply for academic promotion. There are no quotas and all staff meeting the
promotion Guideline can advance. Contributions to teaching, research and community service
are all highly valued.

Instrumentation and Process Control
S/
N
1

Equipment
Description
Circuit
Evaluator
IDL-800A

Function

Picture

It functions as a training unit
designed to evaluate, test, and
analyze electronic circuits. In the
laboratory, it is used to practice
circuit building, troubleshooting,
and performance evaluation,

423

allowing students to strengthen their
understanding of electronics
principles.
2

Control
System Lab

It functions as an experimental
platform for studying control theory
and system dynamics. In the
laboratory, it is used for modeling,
analyzing, and testing control
systems such as feedback loops,
stability analysis, and automation
processes.

3

Digital IC
Tester Dict-01

It functions as a device that tests the
logical operation and performance of
digital integrated circuits. In the
laboratory, it is used for verifying IC
functionality, identifying faulty
chips, and ensuring the correct
operation of digital circuits.

424

4

Oscilloscope
GDS-1052-U

It functions as a measuring
instrument that displays voltage
signals as waveforms on a screen. In
the laboratory, it is used for
observing AC/DC signals, measuring
frequency and amplitude, and
troubleshooting electronic circuits.

5

Multimeter
GDM-451 &
P37772

It functions as a multi-purpose
measuring tool for voltage, current,
resistance, and continuity. In the
laboratory, it is used for diagnostics,
checking circuit conditions, and
verifying measurements in both
electrical and electronic systems.

6

Sensor
Bundles

They function as a set of different
sensors (such as temperature, light,
motion, and pressure) for
experimentation. In the laboratory,
they are used for learning sensor
principles, data collection, and
interfacing sensors with

ANNEX A: KNOWLEDGE PROFILE AND DEFINITION OF
COMPLEX ENGINEERING PROBLEM

455

Annex A-1: Knowledge Attribute Profile
The curriculum shall encompass the knowledge profile as summarised in the table below:
TableA-1: Knowledge Attribute Profile
S/No.

Attribute

K1

A systematic, theory-based understanding of the natural sciences applicable to
the discipline and awareness of relevant social sciences
Conceptually-based mathematics, numerical analysis, data analysis, statistics
and formal aspects of computer and information science to support detailed

K2

analysis and modelling applicable to the discipline

K3

A systematic, theory-based formulation of engineering fundamentals required
in the engineering discipline
Engineering specialist knowledge that provides theoretical frameworks and
bodies of knowledge for the accepted practice areas in the engineering

K4

discipline; much is at the forefront of the discipline
Knowledge, including efficient resource use, environmental impacts, whole-life
cost, re-use of resources, net zero carbon, and similar concepts, that supports

K5

engineering design and operations in a practice area.
Knowledge of engineering practice (technology) in the practice areas in the
engineering discipline

K6
Knowledge of the role of engineering in society and identified issues in
engineering practice in the discipline, such as the professional responsibility of
K7

an engineer to public safety and sustainable development*
Engagement with selected knowledge in the current research literature of the
discipline, awareness of the power of critical thinking and creative approaches

K8

to evaluate emerging issues

456

Ethics, inclusive behaviour and conduct. Knowledge of professional ethics,
responsibilities, and norms of engineering practice. Awareness of the need for
K9

diversity by reason of ethnicity, gender, age, physical ability etc. with mutual
understanding and respect, and of inclusive attitudes
*Represented by the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals (UN-SDG)

457

Annex A-2: Definition of Complex Problem Solving
The range of complex problem solving is defined as follows:
Table A-2: Range of Complex Problem Solving
Attribute

Complex Engineering Problems have
characteristic P1 and some or all of P2 to P7:

Depth of Knowledge Required

P1: Cannot be resolved without in-depth engineering knowledge at the
level of one or more of K3, K4, K5, K6 or K8 which allows a fundamentalsbased, first principles analytical
approach

Range of conflicting requirements P2: Involve wide-ranging and/or conflicting technical, non-technical
issues (such as ethical, sustainability, legal, political, economic, societal)
and consideration of future requirements
Depth of analysis required

P3: Have no obvious solution and require abstract
thinking, creativity and originality in analysis to formulate suitable
models

Familiarity of issues

P4: Involve infrequently encountered issues or
novel problems

Extent of applicable codes

P5: Address problems not encompassed by standards and codes of
practice for professional
engineering

Extent of stakeholder involvement P6: Involve collaboration across engineering disciplines, other fields,
and conflicting requirements

and/or diverse groups of
stakeholders with widely varying needs

458

Interdependence

P7: Address high level problems with many
components or sub-problems that may require a systems approach

459

Annex A-3: Definition of Complex Engineering Activities
The range of complex engineering activities is defined as follows:
Table A-3:

Range of Complex Engineering Activities

Attribute

Complex Activities

Preamble

Complex activities mean (engineering) activities or
projects that have some or all of the following characteristics:

Range of resources

A1: Involve the use of diverse resources including people, data
and information, natural, financial and physical resources and
appropriate technologies including analytical and/or design
software

Level of interactions

A2: Require optimal resolution of interactions between wideranging and/or conflicting technical, non-technical, and
engineering issues

Innovation

A3: Involve creative use of engineering principles, innovative
solutions for a conscious purpose, and research-based
knowledge

Consequences to society and A4: Have significant consequences in a range of contexts,
the environment

characterized by difficulty of prediction and mitigation

Familiarity

A5: Can extend beyond previous experiences by applying
principles-based approaches
